Chapter 1
The academic year 1987–88
Scope and editorial date: 11 November 2026. This chapter covers all 38 cata-
logue records, 714–751, in notebook order, from October 1987 through April 1988.
The archive windows overlap; the Russian text and scans determine the narrower page
attributions. Record 719 precedes 718, and Gelfand’s February exercises (733) pre-
cede Beilinson’s programme (732). The year heading and programme remain records
without invented mathematics. P001460 is a reading list. P001478 explicitly begins
12 September 1988; P001479 continues that next academic year and is excluded, with
the boundary documented in the corrections. The source catalogue is Moscow Center
for Continuous Mathematical Education (2026).
The mathematical year.
Beilinson’s course moves from sheaf cohomology, reso-
lutions and derived categories to microlocal and perverse sheaves, then to Morse
and gauge theory.
Gelfand’s interventions connect symmetric functions, Schubert
polynomials, matroids and Hecke algebras with explicit calculations. Other sustained
strands are dynamical zeta functions, joint stochastic spectra, toric vector bundles,
conformal field theory, principal angles, quantum integrable systems, exceptional
bundles, graph colouring and quantum-group link invariants. Their coexistence is a
feature of the seminar; it does not imply that every juxtaposition is a precise theorem.

The occurrence index has 287 items; the problem index has 27 definite questions or
conjectures. A–F describe historical fate, not mathematical correctness or originality.
The separate assessment fields preserve that distinction. An established theorem can
have a false literal notebook formula, and an unresolved wording can concern a well-
developed subject. No unsuccessful search is promoted into an orphan-idea claim.
What became of the talks.
Exact theorem matching is more informative than
subject matching. Klyachko’s filtration construction led to the 1989 Russian paper
(English translation 1990), submitted in February 1988, including cohomology and
characteristic classes (Klyachko, 1990, Sections 2–4). Conversely, some material that
looks like a subsequent discovery was already circulating: the Schubert-character
construction had a 1986 preprint and 1987 announcement (item 1987-720.21).
Reshetikhin’s reports carry 1987 preprint identifiers, while the scanned editions have
1988 imprints; these are distinguished rather than treated as one date. Publication
chronology is therefore checked against first circulation wherever it could change a
class.

Open questions and the selected development.
The joint stochastic-spectrum
question, GP-1987-21, concerns a whole multiset, not the Karpelevich region for one
eigenvalue.
The development gives complete proofs in dimensions two and three,
structural necessary conditions, and a five-dimensional counterexample to sufficiency
of all power-sum inequalities.
Recent work still addresses the general realization
problem (Johnson and Paparella, 2025). The microlocal reconstruction question, GP-
1987-46, retains the codimension-two essential-image conditions explicitly singled out
in Kashiwara and Vilonen (2014, Problem 9.3); a fully faithful embedding alone is in-
sufficient. The named Riemann hypothesis offers no bounded seminar-specific route.
These latter questions receive precise status entries without manufactured partial
solutions. Three additional annotations repair a bracket that fails Jacobi, settle the
finite-dimensional tensor-angle range, and disprove the notebook’s equivalence be-
tween integral homology-sphere topology and vanishing twisted first cohomology. All
supplied proofs are marked, independently rechecked, and make no novelty claim;
computational output is included.
Continuity and limits.
The Schubert material continues 1985-661.1–8, matroid
representability continues 1985-658.12–16, the Virasoro discussion returns to GP-
1982-03 and 1985-657, and determinant lines connect with 1985-655 and 1985-666.
Earlier occurrence identifiers are retained without pretending that a thematic resem-
blance is the same problem. Primary papers, author manuscripts, Math-Net records
and scans carry the argument; metadata-only checks and unresolved leads remain ex-
plicit. The 85 corrections distinguish mistranscription, translation, ambiguity, faith-
ful mathematical error and provenance. Searches establish the bounded conclusions
recorded here, not the absence of all unpublished work.
1.1
A. A. Beilinson, 1987-10-05: Sheaf cohomology and
the preparation for Verdier duality
record 714; source pages P001385-P001388.
Following the previous notebook’s discussion of sheaves, Beilinson develops sheafi-
fication, exactness, flabby resolutions, and cohomology. A finite-poset exercise con-
nects the general formalism with combinatorial sheaves; the last page begins the
compact-support dual construction needed for Verdier duality. These are foundational
results, with hypotheses suppressed in several classroom formulations.
1987-714.1
The abelian category of sheaves
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P001385-P001386
The notes. Kernels are defined sectionwise and cokernels by associating a sheaf to
the sectionwise cokernel; Exercise 2 identifies kernel of the cokernel with cokernel
of the kernel.
Statement. For a topological space X, sheaves of complex vector spaces form an
abelian category. For f : F →G, (ker f)(U) = ker(F(U) →G(U)), while coker f is the
sheafification of the sectionwise cokernel; coim f ≃im f.
Fate. This is pre-seminar sheaf homological algebra; see Grothendieck (1957, §3)
and the modern sheafification construction in The Stacks Project Authors (2026,
Tag 007X).
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, this remains the standard abelian-category
structure; the sectionwise cokernel need not itself be a sheaf.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
the result; development: none; confidence: high


CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1987–88

1987-714.2
Sheafification and its universal property
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P001385-P001386
The notes. The notes glue compatible local representatives, denote the associated
sheaf by C+, and require unique factorization of every map from C to a sheaf.
Statement. For every presheaf C of complex vector spaces there is a sheaf aC and
a map C →aC such that Hom(aC, F) ∼= Hom(C, F) naturally for every sheaf F; Cx →
(aC)x is an isomorphism for every x.
Reconstruction. The stalk formulation disambiguates the abbreviated gluing de-
scription; a single compatible-family plus construction requires a separated input,
or a second application.
Fate. The Stacks Project Authors (2026, Lemmas 6.17.1–6.17.2 and the following
universal property, Tag 007X) gives the construction. The notation C+ here names
the completed associated sheaf, not necessarily one application of the usual plus
construction.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the universal property uniquely deter-
mines sheafification up to unique isomorphism.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high
1987-714.3
Exactness is tested on stalks
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P001386
The notes. Exercise 3 says a sequence of sheaves is exact if and only if all its stalk
sequences are exact.
Statement. A sequence F1 →F2 →F3 of sheaves of vector spaces on X is exact at
F2 if and only if (F1)x →(F2)x →(F3)x is exact for every x ∈X.
Fate. Sheafification preserves stalks, and filtered colimits of vector spaces are ex-
act; this is part of the classical sheaf formalism of Grothendieck (1957, §3).
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the statement holds without separation or
local-compactness assumptions on the space.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
the result; development: none; confidence: high
1987-714.4
Flabby sheaves and the local-system caveat
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P001386
The notes. A sheaf is called flabby when every section on an open subset extends to
all of X. The following sentence says that locally constant sheaves are not flabby.
Statement. F is flabby if F(X) →F(U) is surjective for every open U ⊂X, equiv-
alently all open restriction maps are surjective.
A nonzero constant sheaf on a
connected locally connected space with a disconnected open subset is not flabby.
The zero sheaf and every sheaf on a discrete space give exceptions to a blanket
claim about local systems.
Reconstruction. The extra topological and nonzero hypotheses state the situation
illustrated by the notebook drawing.
Fate. The definition and acyclicity are classical; The Stacks Project Authors (2026,
Definition 20.12.1 and Lemma 20.12.3, Tag 09SV). The exceptions follow directly
from the definition.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the definition is correct, but the unquali-
fied assertion about locally constant sheaves is false.
Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: exact; verifica-
tion: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1987-714.5
Sheaves and flabby sheaves on a finite poset
Class E
Kind; source. problem; P001386-P001387
The notes. Problem 4 equips a finite poset P with {x} = {y : y ≤x}, asks for sheaves
as representations, and asks which are flabby.
Statement. With upper sets open, a sheaf on finite P is equivalently a covariant
functor F : P →VectC. Its sections on an upper set U are lim
←−−x∈U F(x). It is flabby
exactly when F(x) →lim
←−−y>x F(y) is surjective for every x ∈P, with the empty limit
zero.
Reconstruction. The inverse-limit criterion and its two-direction argument are our
explicit solution of the recorded exercise. [proved here] The stated short argument
is supplied in this companion.
Fate. The diagram description is elementary sheaf theory; the stated flabbiness
test is reconstructed directly: extend a compatible section over an upper set by
adding its remaining points in reverse order. Necessity follows by restricting from
{y : y ≥x} to {y : y > x}. Compare the definition in The Stacks Project Authors
(2026, Tag 09SV).
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the exercise has this finite and effective
answer; surjectivity on individual comparable pairs alone is insufficient at branch-
ing points.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: partial; verification:
read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high
1987-714.6
Combinatorial sheaves and the poset of strata
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P001387
The notes. The map sending a point to its stratum is said to induce an equivalence
from sheaves on the poset of strata to combinatorial sheaves on X.
Statement. For a finite regular cell complex with its face poset, cellular sheaves can
be encoded by vector spaces on cells and compatible maps along face inclusions.
For an arbitrary stratification the stated equivalence requires additional hypothe-
ses: its exact truth depends on what is meant here by a combinatorial sheaf and on
incidence and monodromy data.
Reconstruction. The regular-cell formulation is a limited modern interpretation, not
a claim that the notebook imposed that condition.
Fate. The finite-poset assertion is precise, but the notes refer back to definitions in
a previous notebook. The present pages do not provide the assumptions needed to
identify the claimed equivalence for all stratifications; the general sheaf formalism
in Grothendieck (1957, §3) does not supply them automatically.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, a standard cellular interpretation is avail-
able, while the unrestricted stratified-space claim cannot be certified from this
record alone.
Assessment. literal statement: not determinable; publication match: partial; verifi-
cation: indirect; development: none; confidence: medium
1987-714.7
Exactness of sections when the kernel is flabby
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P001387
The notes. The left-exact global-section functor becomes exact on a short exact
sequence when its first term is flabby.
Statement. If 0 →F →G →H →0 is exact and F is flabby, then 0 →Γ(X, F) →
Γ(X, G) →Γ(X, H) →0 is exact.

Fate. The Stacks Project Authors (2026, Lemma 20.12.3 and its proof, Tag 09SV)
proves precisely the needed lifting property.
It belongs to the older flabby-
resolution method.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the assertion is valid on every topological
space.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
the result; development: none; confidence: high
1987-714.8
Flabby resolutions and sheaf cohomology
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P001387-P001388
The notes. Every sheaf embeds in a flabby one; iterating on cokernels gives a reso-
lution, and its global-section cohomology is independent of the resolution.
Statement. For every sheaf F, the map into the sheaf U 7→Q
x∈U Fx is injective and
the target is flabby. Iteration gives 0 →F →I0 →I1 →· · ·. The groups Hi(Γ(X, I•))
are independent of a chosen flabby resolution and equal RiΓ(X, F).
Reconstruction. The product-of-stalks construction makes the asserted embedding
explicit; independence is via the acyclic-resolution comparison theorem.
Fate. Grothendieck (1957, §3) supplies the derived-functor framework; flabby
acyclicity is checked in The Stacks Project Authors (2026, Lemma 20.12.3, Tag
09SV). This is exposition, not a new 1987 construction.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the resolution computes sheaf cohomology
in all nonnegative degrees.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
the result; development: none; confidence: high
1987-714.9
Cohomology with compact supports
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P001388
The notes. The same resolution construction is proposed with compactly supported
sections on a locally compact space.
Statement. For a locally compact Hausdorff space X, Γc(X, F) consists of sections
with compact support, and Hi
c(X, F) = RiΓc(X, F). A resolution by c-soft sheaves, in
particular an appropriate flabby resolution, computes these groups.
Reconstruction. Hausdorffness and the acyclicity condition are supplied explicitly.
Fate. This is the standard compact-support construction underlying the duality for-
malism already presented in Hartshorne (1966, Chapters II–III); only the publisher
chapter metadata was reopened for that older volume.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the locally compact Hausdorff setting gives
the usual compact-support theory; unrestricted support claims require a specified
family of supports.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: publisher metadata checked; development: none; confidence:
high
1987-714.10
The dual of compactly supported sections
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P001388
The notes. The last construction sets F∗(V ) = Γc(V, F)∗, with restrictions dual to
extension by zero, and relates it to flabbiness.
Statement. For sheaves of complex vector spaces on locally compact Hausdorff
X, set DcF(U) = HomC(Γc(U, F), C). If F is c-soft, compact-support Mayer–Vietoris
makes this presheaf a sheaf. Its restriction maps are surjective because linear func-
tionals extend from vector subspaces, so it is flabby.


CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1987–88
6
Reconstruction. The sufficient c-soft hypothesis and the Mayer–Vietoris explanation
specify the missing sheaf condition.
Fate. This is the elementary compact-support dual used in derived duality; com-
pare the 1966 duality framework (Hartshorne, 1966, Chapters II–III). It is not an
assertion that naive pointwise dualization of an arbitrary sheaf is Verdier duality.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the construction is valid under the stated
acyclicity condition; derived duality requires applying it to a suitable complex.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: indirect; development: none; confidence: high

I. M. Gelfand, 1987-10-12: Dyson’s constant term
and Good’s proof
record 715; source pages P001389-P001390.
Gelfand presents the Dyson constant-term identity and the short interpolation
proof attributed in the notebook to a name written as Вуда. The exact recurrence
and bibliographic year identify I. J. Good’s 1970 proof. The final induction ends at
the top of P001390, before Veselov’s heading.

Class E

The notes. Interpolation is applied to a function printed as identically zero, but the
ensuing sum is one and is used to reduce the exponents one at a time.
Statement. For distinct nonzero xi, interpolation of 1 gives P

1.3
A. P. Veselov, 1987-10-12: Quadrics, the Neumann
system, and two Poisson brackets
record 716; source pages P001390-P001391.
Veselov compares geodesics on a quadric with the Neumann oscillator on a sphere
using Knörrer’s time change and Gauss map. He then presents a spectral-operator
interpretation and two Hamiltonian descriptions in dimension three. Gelfand asks an
inverse problem for a rank-one matrix perturbation; its uniqueness needs qualifica-
tions absent from the shorthand question.
1987-716.1
Geodesics and the constrained Neumann equation
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P001390
The notes. The two columns write ¨q = λAq on (Aq, q) = 1, and x′′ = −Ax+ux on |x|2 = 1.
Statement. Let A be positive definite symmetric. A geodesic q(s) on qT Aq = 1, in
arclength, satisfies qss = λAq, where λ = −qT
s Aqs/∥Aq∥2. Motion on the unit sphere in
potential xT Ax/2 satisfies x′′ = −Ax + ux, with u = xT Ax −∥x′∥2.
Reconstruction. Positive definiteness and arclength fix the ellipsoid case and the
signs of the multipliers.
Fate. These constrained equations are classical. The derivation and normalization
are reproduced by Veselov and Wu (2020, §§2–4), whose later work explicitly dis-
tinguishes Knörrer’s 1982 theorem from its own hyperboloid scattering results.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, both equations follow directly by differ-
entiating their constraints.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high
1987-716.2
Knörrer’s Gauss-map correspondence
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P001390
The notes. The notes credit H. Knörrer, 1982: after changing the parameter, x =
Aq/|Aq| takes geodesics into Neumann trajectories.
Statement. For a nonconstant ellipsoid geodesic as above, choose dτ/ds =
p
−λ(s).
Then x(τ) = Aq(τ)/∥Aq(τ)∥satisfies the Neumann equation with matrix A. The image
is a fixed level of an additional Neumann integral; it is not all Neumann trajectories
for the one fixed quadric.
Reconstruction. The explicit time change and restriction on the converse are sup-
plied from the primary later calculation.
Fate. Veselov and Wu (2020, Theorem 4.1, equations (28)–(33)) gives the calculation
and explicitly attributes it to Knörrer 1982. The original DOI was located (Knörrer,
1982), but the original full paper could not be opened.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the forward correspondence is estab-
lished; the extra integral matters for a converse.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1987-716.6
The second bracket fails Jacobi as written
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P001391
The notes. The second table reads {Mi, Mj}2 = εijkakMk, {Mi, pj}2 = εijkakpk, {pi, pj}2 =
εijkMk, with F = p2/2. It is said to give another Hamiltonian description.
Statement. The displayed table is not a Poisson bracket for a general diagonal A:
its Jacobiator on (M1, p1, p2) is (a3 −a2)M2.
Its formal vector field for F = p2/2 is
also the negative of the first recorded flow. One algebraically valid repair keeps
{Mi, Mj}2 = εijkakMk, changes {Mi, pj}2 to εijkajpk, and changes {pi, pj}2 to −εijkMk.
This repaired bracket is Poisson and gives
˙M = Ap × p, ˙p = M × p with the same F.
Reconstruction. The mixed coefficient and the momentum-momentum sign are
changed explicitly; simply reversing time does not cure the failed Jacobi iden-
tity.
[computed here] The independent checks are recorded in the early-record
verification log.
Fate. The scan confirms the literal indices and sign. The Jacobi counterexample, all
coordinate Jacobi identities for the repaired table, and the two Hamiltonian vector
fields are independently calculated in the accompanying script.
The underlying
Neumann system is classical (Veselov and Wu, 2020, §3); that citation is not an exact
source for this chosen repair. The independent associative-matrix construction in
Section 1.39.2 proves Jacobi and compatibility for the repair.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the literal second Hamiltonian description
is false; the displayed repair is a verified reconstruction, not a uniquely established
reading of the intended historical formula.
Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: partial; verifi-
cation: indirect; development: annotated; confidence: high
1.4
A. A. Beilinson, 1987-10-12: Complexes, homotopy,
and localization
record 717; source pages P001392-P001394.
Beilinson moves from poset representations to complexes over an abelian category,
homotopies, and quasi-isomorphisms. Roof diagrams and the Ore construction intro-
duce the derived category. The same localization is revisited on 23 November; these
occurrences are kept separate, with a cross-link rather than treated as new research.
1987-717.1
Combinatorial sheaves as representations, recalled
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P001392
The notes. The opening reminder assigns objects to points of a poset and compatible
morphisms to ordered pairs.
Statement. A representation of a poset P in an abelian category A is a family Fx
with maps fxy : Fx →Fy for x ≤y, satisfying fxx = 1 and fyzfxy = fxz. For finite P with
upper-set topology this is the corresponding cellular sheaf diagram.


CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1987–88
10
Fate. This repeats the finite-poset construction in occurrence 1987-714.5; its el-
ementary sheaf interpretation uses the same restriction and gluing axioms (The
Stacks Project Authors, 2026, Tag 007X).
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, this is an established categorical encoding,
with the direction fixed by the notebook’s topology.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
the result; development: none; confidence: high
1987-717.2
The abelian category of complexes and cohomology
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P001392-P001393
The notes. The notes introduce C(A), bounded variants, differentials, morphisms,
and Hi.
Statement. For an abelian category A, C(A) has cochain complexes (Xi, di), di+1di =
0, and degree-preserving chain maps. It is abelian with kernels and cokernels de-
greewise, and Hi(X) = ker di/ im di−1. The subcategories Cb, C+, C−impose bounded,
bounded-below, or bounded-above terms.
Fate. This predates the seminar; the derived-category account was already pub-
lished in Hartshorne (1966, Chapter I). Modern definitions and cohomology are in
The Stacks Project Authors (2026, Chapters 12–13).
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, these are standard definitions; cohomol-
ogy need not be exact as a functor on complexes.

1987-717.4
Quasi-isomorphisms
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P001393-P001394
The notes. A map inducing isomorphisms in all cohomological degrees is called a
quasi-isomorphism.
Statement. A chain map f : X →Y is a quasi-isomorphism if Hi(f) is an isomorphism
for every integer i. Such maps need not be homotopy equivalences in an arbitrary
abelian category.
Fate. The Stacks Project Authors (2026, §13.11, Lemma 13.11.2) identifies precisely
this multiplicative system, in the classical derived-category construction.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the distinction from homotopy equivalence
is essential and remains unchanged.

1.6
A. A. Beilinson, 1987-10-19: Truncation, mapping
cones, and distinguished triangles
record 718; source pages P001396-P001398.
Beilinson continues the derived-category course after Gelfand’s intervention. He
explains how cohomology in one degree identifies a complex in the derived category,
contrasts this with splitting in several degrees, and introduces mapping cones and
exact triangles. Several signs and the ordering of cone summands require explicit
conventions.

Statement. If Hi(X) = 0 for i ̸= 0, the canonical truncation τ≤0X, with (τ≤0X)0 = ker d0,

1987-718.2
Cohomology objects do not generally split a complex
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P001396-P001397
The notes. The notes warn that several cohomology groups do not determine a com-
plex as their direct sum, and that a splitting, when available, need not be canonical.
Statement. In a general abelian category a complex need not be isomorphic in D(A)
to its graded cohomology with zero differential: the truncation triangles can have
nonzero connecting extension classes. If every short exact sequence in A splits,
every complex is homotopy equivalent to its graded cohomology, although choices
enter a chain-level splitting.
Reconstruction. The extension-class explanation specifies the warning rather than
imputing a particular example to the speaker.
Fate. The distinction is built into the triangle formalism of Hartshorne (1966, Chap-
ter I) and The Stacks Project Authors (2026, §§13.9–13.11).
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, nonformality is a real phenomenon; the
semisimple hypothesis is sufficient for the stated splitting.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: partial; verification:
read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1987-718.3
When is the homotopy category abelian?
Class E
Kind; source. problem; P001397
The notes. The exercise says K(A) is abelian exactly when A is semisimple; the
handwritten explanation speaks of sums of irreducibles.
Statement. For an abelian category A, K(A) is abelian if and only if every short exact
sequence in A splits. In that case it is equivalent to the category of graded objects of
A. Here semisimple means split exact; a decomposition into simple objects requires
the usual additional finiteness or category hypotheses.
Reconstruction. The split-exact formulation avoids silently imposing finite length.
[proved here] The stated short argument is supplied in this companion.
Fate. The implication from split exactness follows by splitting cycles and bound-
aries.
Conversely an abelian triangulated category is split exact, so every mor-
phism has a generalized inverse. For a monomorphism i : U →V in A, apply this
to its degree-zero map to obtain g with igi = i; cancellation in A gives gi = 1U, forc-
ing all exact sequences in A to split. The triangulated structure is checked in The
Stacks Project Authors (2026, Proposition 13.10.3). Earlier occurrence 1971-266.2
discusses the general failure of abelianness.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the exercise is a settled structural crite-
rion, with the meaning of semisimple specified.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: indirect; development: annotated; confidence: high

1987-720.17

Class E

The notes. For n+1 variables, codes satisfy 0 ≤i1 ≤n, . . . , 0 ≤in+1 ≤0; the notes then

1987-720.19
Poincaré pairing and adjoint divided differences
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P001404-P001405
The notes. The pairing is obtained from ∂1∂2∂1(PQ) in three variables; divided dif-

Statement. On the coinvariant algebra for SN, define (P, Q) = ϵ(∂w0(PQ)), where ϵ sets

1987-720.20
Freeness over symmetric polynomials
Class E

The notes. The polynomial ring in three variables is said to be free over its symmet-

Statement. For N ≥1, Z[x1, . . . , xN] is a free module over Z[e1, . . . , eN], with basis

Fate. This is the polynomial form of the coinvariant-basis theorem (Bernstein et al.,

1.8
M. M. Kapranov, 1987-11-09: Double categories,
two-categories, and higher fundamental groupoids
record 721; source pages P001405-P001406.
Kapranov introduces squares with horizontal and vertical composition, the struc-
ture now conventionally called a double category, and its interchange axiom. Kontse-
vich contrasts this with two-morphisms between parallel arrows. A tensor-contraction
example, Gelfand’s boundary-value suggestion, and the homotopy squares of a filtered
space give three distinct motivations.
1987-721.1
Double categories and the interchange law
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P001405-P001406
The notes. The heading says 2-categories, while the data comprise objects, horizon-
tal arrows, vertical arrows, and squares with two compositions.
Statement. A strict double category is a category object in categories: its squares
have horizontal and vertical boundary arrows and associative unital compositions
◦h, ◦v satisfying
(A ◦v B) ◦h (C ◦v D) = (A ◦h C) ◦v (B ◦h D)
whenever the four squares have matching boundaries.
Reconstruction. The modern name separates this structure from the strict 2-
category in the later intervention.
Fate. Brown and Spencer (1976, §1, equations (1.1)–(1.5)) provides these exact four
kinds of data and the interchange law; the paper appeared in 1976.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, this is established double-category termi-
nology; the notebook uses a broader informal label.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
the result; development: none; confidence: high
1987-721.2
Why naive corner-index contraction fails interchange
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P001406
The notes. Four-index tensors are multiplied by contracting the two indices on a
common edge; the note says this example does not satisfy interchange.
Statement. Write a square tensor as A(k, l; i, j). Define horizontal contraction by (A◦h
C)(k, r; i, s) = P
j,l A(k, l; i, j)C(l, r; j, s), and vertical contraction by (A ◦v B)(k, l; u, v) =
P
i,j A(k, l; i, j)B(i, j; u, v). With indices 0, 1, take the only nonzero entries of A, C, B, D
to be, respectively, A(0, 0; 0, 0) = C(0, 0; 0, 0) = B(0, 1; 0, 0) = D(1, 0; 0, 0) = 1. The outer
all-zero entry of (A ◦h C) ◦v (B ◦h D) is 1, while that of (A ◦v B) ◦h (C ◦v D) is 0.
Reconstruction. The full vertical contraction convention and sparse counterexample

Fate. The failure follows by two direct finite contractions, independently checked

1987-721.3
Strict two-categories and Cat
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P001406; M. L. Kontsevich
The notes. Kontsevich proposes morphisms between morphisms with identical end-
points; Cat is the example. The note calls this a special case obtained by collapsing
vertical arrows.
Statement. A strict 2-category has objects, 1-morphisms, and 2-morphisms between
parallel 1-morphisms, with strictly associative unital horizontal and vertical com-
positions satisfying interchange. In Cat, these are categories, functors, and natural
transformations. A strict 2-category embeds as a double category whose vertical
arrows are identities.
Fate. This is pre-1987 higher-category formalism; Brown and Spencer (1976, §1 and
references to Bénabou, Gray, and Kelly–Street) distinguishes the double-category
structure and its specializations.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the two descriptions are related, but their
general definitions are not interchangeable.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: partial; verification:
read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1987-721.5
Homotopy squares of a filtered space
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P001406
The notes. For X0 ⊂X1 ⊂X, vertices lie in X0, edges are paths in X1, and squares
map (I2, ∂I2) to (X, X1), modulo homotopy.
Statement. For a filtered space X0 ⊂X1 ⊂X, take paths in X1 with endpoints in
X0, and squares in X whose edges lie in X1 and vertices in X0. Homotopies must
preserve these filtrations and fix vertices; the resulting homotopy double-groupoid
construction has horizontal and vertical gluing. At a basepoint, its relative structure
is encoded by the crossed module π2(X, X1) →π1(X1).
Reconstruction. The filtered and vertex-fixed homotopy conventions are essential
additions to the shorthand.
Fate. Brown and Spencer (1976, §1, Example 1; introduction; Theorem A) explains
squares, their double compositions, and the crossed-module relation. Theorem A
circulated in a 1972 preliminary report, before its 1976 publication.

25
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the construction is classical once the ho-
motopies and connections are specified; arbitrary unrelative homotopy would not
preserve the necessary boundary data.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:

1.10
I. S. Zakharevich, 1987-11-23: Bernoulli coding, the
baker map, and hyperbolicity
record 723; source pages P001408.
The short talk illustrates sensitivity to finite observational precision using the
two-sided binary shift and the baker transformation. A closing comparison invokes
geodesic flow on a negatively curved manifold. Measure-theoretic coding, topological
conjugacy, and deterministic loss of exact information must be distinguished.
1987-723.1
The two-sided Bernoulli shift and baker map
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P001408
The notes. The notes depict a bi-infinite sequence of zeroes and ones and encode
the two halves as binary coordinates of a square.
Statement. Let Σ = {0, 1}Z with product measure and (σa)j = aj+1. Set x = .a0a1 · · ·,
y = .a−1a−2 · · ·. Outside the null set of ambiguous dyadic expansions, this gives a
measure-preserving conjugacy with
B(x, y) = (2x −⌊2x⌋, (y + ⌊2x⌋)/2).
Reconstruction. The formula, probability measure, and dyadic exception are sup-
plied explicitly.
Fate. The coding is checked directly by moving the binary point. The shift and hyper-
bolic examples belong to the pre-1987 theory surveyed in Smale (1967, hyperbolic
examples); only that survey’s metadata was reopened in this run.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the measure-theoretic conjugacy is exact
almost everywhere; the square and the Cantor product are not globally homeomor-
phic.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: indirect; development: none; confidence: high
1987-723.2
Finite observation and loss of predictive information
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P001408
The notes. Iterating the shift is described as gradually losing information about a
point when only finitely many digits are known.
Statement. For independent fair binary coordinates, if only aj with |j| ≤m are ob-
served, then any unobserved coordinate ak, |k| > m, still has conditional probabil-
ities 1/2, 1/2. Under σn, the observed window shifts, and future digits outside the
initial finite window are not determined by it. The complete two-sided shift remains
invertible.
Reconstruction. Conditional probability is our precise interpretation of the informal
information claim.
Fate. This precise finite-window statement is a direct consequence of product in-
dependence, not a new theorem about destruction of exact state information. It
clarifies the standard symbolic-dynamics example associated with Smale (1967).
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the observation-level assertion is correct;
exact invertible dynamics does not erase its full initial data.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: indirect; development: none; confidence: high

1987-723.3
Negative curvature as a geometric hyperbolicity
example
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P001408
The notes. The closing line compares the binary example with geodesics on a neg-

Statement. If M is a closed smooth Riemannian manifold with strictly negative sec-
tional curvature, its geodesic flow on the unit tangent bundle is Anosov: the tangent

contraction in forward or backward time.

Fate. Anosov (1967) is the original 1967 monograph; its Math-Net publication
record was opened. The notebook does not state a literal conjugacy of this flow to
a binary shift.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, uniform hyperbolicity holds under com-

1.11

record 724; source pages P001409-P001410.
Margulis moves from affine maps and squaring to the Fatou and Julia sets of a

1987-724.1
Affine iteration and invariant circles
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P001409
The notes. For z 7→az + b, the notes say that |a| = 1 gives invariant circles carrying
rotations.
Statement. If a ̸= 1, translating by z∗= b/(1 −a) conjugates the map to w 7→aw.

translation and has no fixed point or invariant Euclidean circle; if a = 1, b = 0, it is
the identity.
Fate. The correction follows by direct substitution and supplies the missing excep-

1987-724.2
Smooth conjugacy of circle diffeomorphisms to
rotations
Class E
Kind; source. question; P001409; I. M. Gelfand
The notes. Gelfand says the circle-conjugacy question is nontrivial and names Den-
joy, Arnold, and Herman.
Statement. An orientation-preserving C2 circle diffeomorphism with irrational ro-
tation number is topologically conjugate to the corresponding rotation. If it is C∞
and its rotation number satisfies a Diophantine condition |α−p/q| ≥Cq−2−ν for some
C, ν > 0 and all rational p/q, the conjugacy can be chosen C∞. Smoothness alone
does not ensure a smooth conjugacy for every irrational rotation number.
Reconstruction. The modern formulation separates topological and smooth conju-
gacy, which the notes do not spell out.
Fate. Yoccoz (1984, Introduction and main theorem) proves the smooth Diophantine
statement, generalizing Herman, in 1984. It predates this intervention.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the regularity and arithmetic qualifica-
tions are essential; the remark is about an established theory, not an explicitly new
conjecture.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: partial; verification:
read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1987-724.5
Hausdorff dimension of the Cantor set
Class E
Kind; source. problem; P001409; I. M. Gelfand
The notes. Gelfand asks for the Hausdorff dimension of the perfect Cantor set, with-
out spelling out its construction.
Statement. For the middle-thirds Cantor set C, defined by C = 1
3C∪( 2
3+ 1
3C), dimH C =
log 2/ log 3, and its Hausdorff measure in that dimension is finite and positive. An
arbitrary perfect nowhere-dense subset of the line need not have this dimension.
Reconstruction. Middle thirds is the conventional interpretation, explicitly marked

Fate. Hutchinson (1981, §5.3(1), with the examples in §3.3) gives the self-similar
dimension theorem. The paper appeared as a 1979 ANU report before its 1981

1987-724.6
Squaring and one-sided binary dynamics
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P001409
The notes. For z 7→z2, the unit circle is invariant, the inside tends to zero, the outside

Statement. If |z| < 1, z2n →0; if |z| > 1, it tends to infinity on the Riemann sphere. On

1987-724.7
The Fatou set and the Julia set
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P001410
The notes. The Fatou set is the open set of local normality of the iterates, and its
closed complement is the Julia set; the family is called compact.
Statement. For a rational map R : bC →bC of degree at least two, F(R) consists of

J(R) = bC \ F(R) is closed.

relative sequential compactness in this topology, not that the set of iterates is itself
closed and compact.
Fate. Sullivan (1985, p. 403) states this stable-set definition and its relation to Fa-

Class E

1987-724.9
Sullivan: no wandering Fatou components
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P001410
The notes. Sullivan is credited with showing that every component orbit stabilizes
and that there are finitely many fixed components.
Statement. For every rational map R of degree at least two and every Fatou com-
ponent U, there exist integers m ≥0, p ≥1 such that Rm+p(U) = Rm(U). There are
finitely many periodic component cycles. Eventual periodicity does not mean that
every component eventually becomes fixed under R.
Fate. Sullivan (1985, Theorem 1, p. 404) states eventual periodicity exactly; the fol-
lowing discussion treats the finite periodic types. The article appeared in Septem-
ber 1985, before the seminar.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the no-wandering-domain theorem is es-

1987-724.10
The quasiconformal deformation argument
Class E
Kind; source. method; P001410
The notes. The proof sketch supposes an infinite orbit of components and changes
the conformal structures while keeping the map conformal.
Statement. The strategy is to assign compatible Beltrami data along a hypothetical
wandering orbit, integrate it by the measurable Riemann mapping theorem, and ob-
tain too many independent quasiconformal deformations for the finite-dimensional
space of rational maps of the fixed degree. Producing the independent deforma-
tions and controlling conjugacy is part of the proof, not a consequence of parameter
counting alone.
Fate. Sullivan (1985, §§7–10) implements the method. The introduction dates ear-
lier lectures to 1978, 1980–81, and 1981; the final article is 1985.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the notebook accurately signals the proof
mechanism but does not record a complete proof.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1987-725.1
Vector matroids and symmetric basis exchange
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P001411
The notes. The independent subfamilies of a finite vector configuration define its
matroid. A displayed axiom permits simultaneous exchange in two bases.
Statement. Let E be finite. A nonempty family B of equal-cardinality subsets is a
matroid basis family if, for B1, B2 ∈B and y ∈B2 \ B1, there exists x ∈B1 \ B2 such
that both B1 −x+y and B2 −y +x belong to B. This symmetric exchange formulation


CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1987–88
32
is equivalent to the usual basis axiom. For a vector configuration, the bases are its
maximal independent subfamilies.
Reconstruction. Nonemptiness, equal cardinality, and exclusion of the exchanged
element from the other basis are explicit.
Fate. This is classical matroid theory; the representation and independence defini-
tions are stated in Oxley (n.d., Theorem 1.6 and §1), while the basis-based bimatroid
reformulation is recalled in Röhrle and Ulirsch (2026, Proposition 2.2). It repeats
the subject of occurrence 1985-658.12.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the finite nonempty-family and equal-
cardinality conventions make the displayed axiom precise.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1987-725.3
Which finite matroids are real or complex
representable?
Class E
Kind; source. problem; P001411
The notes. The notebook calls the general question of representability over R or C
unsolved, without specifying a type of classification.
Statement. For a finite rank-r matroid on [n] with basis list B, representability over
k = R or C is equivalent to the existence of A ∈kr×n satisfying det AB ̸= 0 for B ∈
B, and det AI = 0 for every other r-subset I. These are finitely many polynomial
conditions, and their solvability is decidable over both fields.
Reconstruction. The finite-input decision interpretation is made explicit; it is not
claimed to supply an efficient or illuminating combinatorial classification.
Fate. Tarski (1951, §2, pp. 15–42, and §3, pp. 43–46) explicitly states quantifier
elimination over the reals and its complex extension. The nonvanishing conditions
can equivalently be encoded by an auxiliary equation u Q
B∈B det AB = 1. Thus the
finite decision problem was settled before 1987.
Occurrences 1985-658.13 and
1985-658.16 already connect representability with polynomial systems.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, a finite decision procedure exists; the
source specifies no stronger structural classification or complexity target that could
responsibly be declared open.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high;
problem GP-1987-02

The notes. The notes say that representability over every field has been character-

1987-725.5
The bimatroid of nonzero minors
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P001411-P001412
The notes. For a matrix with row set E and column set F, the notes retain all pairs

Statement. For a matrix A ∈kE×F , define R(A) = {(I, J) : I ⊆E, J ⊆F, |I| =

1987-725.6
An axiom system for bimatroids
Class E
Kind; source. problem; P001411-P001412
The notes. The explicit question asks for axioms on the collection of nonzero square
minors.
Statement. For finite disjoint E, F, a collection R of pairs (I, J) with |I| = |J| is an
abstract bimatroid exactly when {(E \ I) ⊔J : (I, J) ∈R} is a matroid basis family
on E ⊔F containing E. Thus nonemptiness, the pair (∅, ∅), and the ordinary basis-
exchange axiom after this substitution are a complete axiom system. In the matrix
case the extended matroid is represented by [IE | A].
Fate. Röhrle and Ulirsch (2026, Proposition 2.2 and Example 2.3) proves this equiv-
alence in the exposition of Kung’s 1978 theory. This is a later checked source for
an older answer, not a 2026 solution date.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the recorded axiomatization problem is
solved by an explicit exchange system; abstract bimatroids need not be realizable
matrices.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
the result; development: annotated; confidence: high; problem GP-1987-03

1987-726.3
The modular analogy and exceptional parameters
Class E

The notes. Dipper–James is recommended under the handwritten name Tipper; the
theory is called analogous to modular symmetric-group representations, even over

Statement. For q ∈C×, define e(q) as the least positive integer e with 1 + q + · · · +

1987-726.4
Young diagrams, tableaux, and axial adjacency
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P001413-P001414
The notes. Diagrams are partitions, written with column lengths. A tableau is an
increasing chain of diagrams, and its axial distance compares the cells containing
k and k + 1.
Statement. A standard tableau of shape λ ⊢N is a chain ∅= λ(0) ⊂λ(1) ⊂· · · ⊂λ(N) =
λ, with one cell added each time. If cell k has row-column coordinates (ik, jk), set
rk(t) = (ik+1 −jk+1) −(ik −jk), 1 ≤k < N. Say s ∼k t when their chains agree except
possibly at stage k. For N ≥2, the recorded axial parameter is h(λ) = 1+maxt,k |rk(t)|.
Fate. Kerov (1987, §2, p. 157) gives the same definitions with k < N. The notebook’s
upper limit k ≤N refers to a nonexistent cell N + 1. Partitions also classify conju-
gacy classes of SN by cycle lengths, but that labeling is distinct from the tableau
construction.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, the chain and axial-distance definitions
are standard once the index range and row-column convention are fixed.

Class E

the distinct adjacent tableau, and zero otherwise, taking the nonnegative square

satisfies the Hecke relations and realizes the corresponding Specht module. The
limit q = 1 recovers Young’s orthogonal form. Generic complex parameters also
work with coherent square-root choices.
Reconstruction. The positive-real case supplies an unambiguous square-root con-
vention; the broader generic-complex statement requires the convention in the orig-
inal construction.
Fate. Kerov (1987, §2, Definition 1, Theorem 1 and Remarks 1–2, pp. 157–158)
proves the representation. The notebook expressly introduces this as a report on
Kerov’s existing work. Its journal date is 1987; an exact earlier circulation day was
not established.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026, this is an established construction already
being expounded; arbitrary independent complex square-root choices should not be
substituted for the coherent convention.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1987-728.3
Single-eigenvalue constraints for a stochastic matrix
Class E
Kind; source. remark; P001418; I. M. Gelfand
The notes. Gelfand recalls that a stochastic matrix has eigenvalue 1 and spectrum
in the unit disk, and mentions work of Dynkin and Dmitriev on the possible position
of an individual eigenvalue.
Statement. For a real row-stochastic n × n matrix P, P1 = 1 and ρ(P) ≤∥P∥∞= 1;
nonreal eigenvalues occur in conjugate pairs. Determining the union of possible
individual eigenvalues projects away all correlations between eigenvalues and is a
different question from realizing a whole multiset.
Fate. The elementary bound follows directly from the stated stochastic hypothe-
ses. The exact single-eigenvalue region was determined by Karpelevich (1951); its
opened record confirms publication in1951, received19May1950. The source at-
tribution to Dynkin and Dmitriev concerns earlier work and is retained. The joint
problem is assessed separately in item1987-728.4.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: These elementary necessary conditions
are known and are far from a sufficient joint-spectrum criterion.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: partial; verification:
publisher metadata checked; development: none; confidence: high

j=1(t −λj). Multiplicity

1987-729.10
The determinant-divisor formula has the wrong
variance
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P001422
The notes. For D = P aixi, the notes define det FD = N
i(det Fxi)ai, then write
det H•(X, F) = det FK for the canonical divisor K.
Statement. The divisor construction is meaningful for a local system F and a spec-
ified divisor. The claimed natural identity with K is false under the immediately
preceding alternating determinant convention. For a constant local system V on
a compact genus-g curve, automorphisms of V act on det RΓ(X, V ) with determi-
nant weight 2 −2g, while det VK has weight 2g −2. Dualizing the right side removes
this elementary sign obstruction, but a canonical general product formula requires
additional local epsilon data and conventions.
Reconstruction. The comparison of determinant weights is our consistency check.
We do not silently replace the source formula by an asserted canonical isomorphism
with F−K.
Fate. Knudsen and Mumford (1976, Section I) fixes the determinant convention;
Laumon (1987, Chapter III, Theorem 3.2.1.1) proves a precise arithmetic epsilon-
product statement, not this unqualified identity of lines.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: The literal formula is false functorially.
The sign diagnosis is decisive even on a constant sheaf on the projective line; it
does not establish a replacement theorem for arbitrary constructible sheaves.

1987-729.11
Laumon: local factors for global epsilon constants
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P001422
The notes. The discussion of determinants is presented as a report on Laumon’s
work in number theory and on a local description of the functional-equation con-
stant.
Statement. For a smooth proper curve over a finite field of characteristic p and
a constructible l-adic complex, l ̸= p, the global epsilon constant of its L-function
is expressed as a product of locally defined epsilon factors, with the normalization
and genus/rank factor specified by Laumon. A nonzero rational differential supplies

Reconstruction. The arithmetic objects and hypotheses come from the identified

Fate. Laumon (1987, Theorem 3.2.1.1; Remark 3.2.1.3) contains the exact product

normalization hypotheses matter: this result cannot be read as a blanket complex-

1.17
A. A. Klyachko, 1987-12-07: Equivariant toric
bundles and compatible filtrations
record 730; source pages P001422-P001424.
Klyachko associates decreasing filtrations to invariant divisors of a toric variety
and states a compatibility criterion, then asks for cohomology, characteristic classes
and classification. Simplicial-complex examples lead to the triangle and tetrahedral
boundary. His 1989 paper, submitted in February 1988, supplies the precise smooth
and singular compatibility conditions and geometric calculations.
The tetrahedral
decomposition requires the short-filtration restriction that the notebook omits.
1987-730.1
Toric varieties, fans, and equivariant vector bundles
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P001422-P001423
The notes. The speaker defines a toric variety by an algebraic-torus action with
an orbit isomorphic to the torus, associates a fan, and defines a compatible linear
action on a vector bundle.
Statement. A normal toric variety over an algebraically closed field contains T ≃
(Gm)d as a dense open orbit, with multiplication extending to the variety. It corre-
sponds to a rational polyhedral fan in NR, where N = Hom(Gm, T) is the cocharacter
lattice. A toric vector bundle is an algebraic vector bundle equipped with a T-action
linear on fibres and covering the base action.
Reconstruction. Density, normality and the integral cocharacter structure are sup-
plied.
Fate. This is the standard background recalled by Klyachko (1990, Section 1). The
existence of an orbit merely isomorphic to T is not enough without density and
normality.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: Established definitions. The combina-
torial lattice belongs to one-parameter subgroups, not an arbitrary vector-space
identification with the Lie algebra.

1987-730.4
Classification and moduli of compatible filtration
data
Class B
Kind; source. problem; P001423
The notes. The second broad task is classification of the bundles; the speaker then
abstracts the compatibility rule to a finite simplicial complex with a filtration at
every vertex.
Statement. For a fixed complex K, a fixed rank r and prescribed dimensions of the
filtration steps, classify the configurations of flags in Cr whose subspaces are simul-
taneously split on each simplex, modulo simultaneous GLr. For a toric fan, use the
stronger character compatibility when needed. Constructing a parameter space
and understanding its orbit equivalence is a precise interpretation of classification;
it need not produce a finite list.
Reconstruction. Rank, flag dimensions and the quotient problem are supplied; the
paper cited solves the toric fixed-data moduli problem, not every possible meaning
of classification.
Fate. For toric bundles, Payne (2008, Theorem 1.1 and Section 4), first circulated
in May 2007 and published in 2008, constructs framed moduli with fixed equivari-
ant Chern data and proves Murphy’s law already in rank three. This develops the
geometric moduli question, while not claiming a simple orbit list for every abstract
simplicial complex.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: Established moduli constructions and
severe singularity phenomena explain the limits of a uniform elementary classifica-
tion. The arbitrary-complex reformulation remains a representation problem with
complexity depending on K, not one unqualified open conjecture.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high; problem

1987-730.6
Tetrahedral configurations and the short-filtration
restriction
Class A
Kind; source. claim; P001423-P001424
The notes. For the boundary of a tetrahedron the notes give four planes in general
position in a three-dimensional space, their exterior powers, and assert that all
representations split into those and one-dimensional objects.
Statement. The matching theorem concerns toric bundles on projective space
whose ray filtrations are short: each has at most one proper nonzero subspace,
with allowed integer shifts. For P3, such bundles decompose into line bundles and
the appropriate twists of exterior powers of its cotangent bundle. Their filtration
configurations include the four general-position planes described in the notes. The
same conclusion for arbitrary long filtrations does not follow.
Reconstruction. The short-filtration hypothesis is taken from the proved theorem.
The source does not state it explicitly, so the literal unrestricted assertion is not
endorsed.
Fate. Klyachko (1990, Theorem 6.4.1) states the required short-filtration theorem;
published 1989/1990 after its December 1987 exposition. The missing restriction
is substantive.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: The restricted theorem is established.

projective three-space or of all arbitrary-length tetrahedral flag configurations.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:

1987-733.3
Discontinuities of a pointwise limit of continuous
functions
Class E
Kind; source. problem; P001425
The notes. Problem3 asks whether a pointwise limit on an interval can be discon-
tinuous, have a dense set of discontinuities, or be discontinuous everywhere.
Statement. A real-valued pointwise limit of continuous functions on a nondegener-
ate real interval is Baire class1 and has a dense G-delta set of continuity points. It
may be discontinuous (for example xn on [0, 1]), and discontinuities may be dense
(Thomae’s function, with value 1/q at a reduced rational p/q and0 at irrationals). It
cannot be discontinuous everywhere.
Fate. Baire (1900, p. 173, theorem and reference to the1899 thesis) provides the
classical criterion for pointwise continuous approximation. The category conclusion
applies to interval domains, which are Baire spaces.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: All three questions are settled: yes, yes,
no. Pointwise convergence must not be replaced by uniform convergence.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
the result; development: none; confidence: high; problem GP-1987-26
1987-733.4
Conformally map the radially slit disk
Class E
Kind; source. problem; P001425
The notes. Problem4(a) draws a disk cut along a radius and asks for a conformal
map to a disk.
Statement. For Ω= {z : |z| < 1} \ [0, 1), choose w = √z with 0 < arg z < 2π. Then w
maps onto the upper half-disk; h = (1 + w)/(1 −w) maps that onto the first quadrant.
Therefore f(z) = (h2 −i)/(h2 + i) is a biholomorphism from Ωto the unit disk. A
rotated slit is treated by an initial rotation.
Reconstruction. The unit radius and positive-real slit choose coordinates for the
picture; the construction is supplied here.
Fate. The existence is a classical case of the mapping theorem originating in Rie-
mann (1851, pp. 37–39 of the checked transcription). The elementary displayed
composition is an annotation of the exercise, not a formula found in the notebook.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: Solved. The slit endpoint0 is removed, so
no normalization f(0)=0 is imposed.

1987-733.5
Conformally map the dumbbell domain with a thin
corridor
Class E
Kind; source. problem; P001425
The notes. Problem4(b) draws two unit disks joined by a corridor of width 10−6,
marks A at a disk centre, and asks for a disk map sending A to0.
Statement. If the drawn union is an open connected simply connected proper plane
domain Ωand A ∈Ω, the Riemann mapping theorem gives a biholomorphism f :
Ω→D with f(A) = 0; fixing f′(A) > 0 makes it unique. Corridor width alone does
not determine Ω: its length, boundary shape and junctions are also needed for a
particular numerical formula.
Reconstruction. The topological hypotheses and derivative normalization are stated
explicitly; no unseen corridor length is invented.
Fate. This is the same established mapping theorem as in Riemann (1851). Thin
corridors can make numerical conformal parametrization poorly conditioned, but
the notes give no quantitative estimate to verify.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: Existence and normalized uniqueness are
settled. A unique explicit numerical map cannot be extracted from the incomplete
geometric dimensions in the sketch.

1.21
D. V. Alekseevsky, 1988-02-08: G-structures, first
jets, connections and torsion
record 734; source pages P001426-P001428.
The lecture develops reductions of the frame bundle, their tensor examples and sol-
der forms. First jets of sections become horizontal complements, giving connections
and a torsion map. These are standard constructions, not a new existence theorem
for canonical connections. The source contains two useful corrections: the principal
action is transitive on each fibre, and the vertical tangent quotient belongs to P, not
to M.

A G-structure is a principal reduction

The notes. The first definition takes a principal subbundle P ⊂F(M) for G ⊂GL(V ),

Statement. For a smooth n-manifold M and closed Lie subgroup G ⊂GL(n, R), a G-

1987-734.2
Tensor fields of constant algebraic type define
reductions
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P001426
The notes. The examples include a metric, a tensor in one canonical form,
an endomorphism with fixed Jordan form, an almost complex structure and a
nondegenerate2-form.
Statement. If a smooth tensor field takes values in one GL(n)-orbit and its adapted
frames form a smooth reduction, its stabilizer is the structure group. A positive
metric yields O(n); J2 = −I in dimension2m yields GL(m, C); a nondegenerate
alternating2-form yields Sp(2m, R). An almost symplectic form becomes symplectic
only with the additional differential condition dω = 0. An endomorphism requires
locally constant algebraic type for its adapted frames.
Reconstruction. The orbit/reduction smoothness and even-dimensional require-
ments are made explicit.
Fate. These are the elementary G-structure examples in the framework of Alek-
seevsky and Michor (1995, Section 5.1); the note itself distinguishes almost sym-
plectic from symplectic.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: Established. A pointwise tensor classifi-
cation does not imply differential integrability of the resulting structure.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:

1987-734.5
A principal connection as an equivariant horizontal
distribution
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P001427-P001428
The notes. The space of connections is written as the G-equivariant sections of P′ →
P; horizontal lifts give parallel transport along curves.
Statement. A principal connection is a smooth G-equivariant assignment p 7→Hp

vertical fields and satisfying R∗
gω = Ad(g−1)ω. The horizontal-lift ODE defines par-

connections it extends along compact parameter intervals by local trivializations.
Fate. The principal-connection framework is recalled by Alekseevsky and Michor

of connections; it does not prove that torsion can always be set to zero.

The notes. Gelfand asks what should be called the angle between two two-
dimensional planes in four-dimensional Euclidean space.
Statement. For two linear two-planes U, W in a four-dimensional real inner-product
space, choose orthonormal frame matrices QU, QW . The singular values of QT
UQW
are cos θ1, cos θ2, where 0 ≤θ1 ≤θ2 ≤π/2. This ordered pair is a complete invariant
under the full orthogonal group, and every pair in that range occurs.
Reconstruction. The word planes is interpreted as linear subspaces and the acting
group as the full orthogonal group, consistently with the following problem sheet.
Fate. The singular-value characterization was already explicit in Björck and Golub
(1973, Section 3, Theorem 1), published in 1973. The earlier index records related
angle questions in 1964-46.2. The same question is restated as problem 3 of record
737.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: Solved classical finite-dimensional classi-
fication. One scalar angle loses information in this dimension.

1987-736.2

Class E

The notes. The fields form an algebra with Ai(x)Aj(y) = P
k c k

Statement. If the chosen field system has convergent OPE expansions in correla-

1987-736.4
Reflection positivity requires a full quadratic form
Class E

The notes. For the reflection ϑ(x1, x2) = (−x1, x2), the matrix ⟨A+
i (ϑx)Aj(x)⟩is required
to be positive definite for every x.
Statement. The Euclidean positivity axiom is positive semidefiniteness of ⟨(ΘF)∗F⟩
for every finite linear combination F of smeared field products supported in one
open time half-space.
The reflected form can have null vectors, which are quo-
tiented out in Hilbert-space reconstruction. Positivity of the displayed same-point
matrix is only a restricted necessary test and does not by itself imply the full axiom.

quotient are missing in the notes.
Fate. The opened record for Osterwalder and Schrader (1975, abstract) confirms
the corrected Euclidean reconstruction theorem; full-text retrieval was blocked, so
no claim is made to have read that proof. The full quadratic-form condition is the
explicit modern reconstruction used here.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: Established framework. Strict pointwise
positivity is too strong in the presence of null fields and too weak as a substitute for
reflection positivity on all field products.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:

1987-736.6
Conservation and tracelessness give chiral stress
components
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P001431
The notes. With z = x1 + ix2, the notebook defines T = T zz, ¯T = T ¯z¯z, sets T z¯z = 0, then
asserts ∂¯zT = ∂z ¯T = 0.
Statement. For a genuine contravariant symmetric tensor, T zz = T 11 −T 22 + 2iT 12
and T z¯z = T 11 + T 22. Conservation and zero trace imply ∂zT zz = 0, so the displayed
contravariant T has the opposite chirality. The conventional holomorphic compo-
nent is the covariant Tzz = (T 11 −T 22 −2iT 12)/4, satisfying ∂¯zTzz = 0. Rescaling this
component gives the normalization used in the OPE.
Reconstruction. This calculation distinguishes contravariant from covariant compo-
nents rather than silently using the same symbol for both.
Fate. The holomorphic stress-tensor construction is standard in Belavin et al. (1984,
Section 2, equations (2.6)–(2.10)). Direct tensor transformation and the two real
conservation equations expose the notebook index inconsistency.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: The standard chiral conclusion is cor-
rect after an explicit index/chirality correction. The separate equality T z¯z = T µµ on
P001433 is consistent with contravariant indices and ds2 = dz d¯z; it should not be
independently changed by a factor of two.
Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: partial; verifi-
cation: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high

The two commuting Virasoro algebras

The notes. The boxed formula is [Ln, Lm] = (n −m)Ln+m +
c
12(n3 −n)δn+m,0, and an

Statement. Taking residues in the stress OPE yields the displayed commutator, with
the central term multiplying the identity. The antiholomorphic modes satisfy the
analogous relation, and left and right modes commute in the local theory. Modulo

extension or projective representation of the diffeomorphism group, not simply the
unextended group.

GP-1982-03 is not relabelled as a new problem here.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: Established algebraic relation. The re-
striction to n = −1, 0, 1 has zero central term and recovers the projective conformal
subalgebra.

Complete invariants called angles: a problem sheet
record 737; source pages P001435-P001437.
The handout defines an angle to mean a complete orbit invariant, including its at-
tainable values. It passes from Euclidean pairs and configurations under GL, through
indefinite quadratic spaces, to a tensor construction and closed Hilbert subspaces.
The finite Euclidean and Hilbert questions were already classical; finite indefinite
pairs have a later precise canonical-form treatment. An indefinite infinite-dimensional
version is too underspecified to classify as an open problem.

1987-737.1
An angle invariant for a line and a plane in three
dimensions
Class E
Kind; source. problem; P001435
The notes. The preamble calls an angle a complete orbit invariant and asks for its
attainable values. Problem 1 asks for the angle between a line and a plane in three-
dimensional space, explicitly separating intersecting and nonintersecting cases.
Statement. For an affine line ℓand an affine plane H under all Euclidean isometries,
the direction angle α ∈[0, π/2], together with their distance δ ≥0, is complete, with
the restriction α > 0 ⇒δ = 0. When α = 0, delta distinguishes a contained line
from a disjoint parallel line. For linear subspaces only δ = 0 occurs. Under origin-
preserving orthogonal maps alone, additional positional data are needed for affine
objects.
Reconstruction. Allowing translations is an explicit interpretation of the noninter-
secting case, not an unannounced change from the preamble about orthogonal
maps.
Fate. The directional angle is the elementary case of the singular-value charac-
terization in Björck and Golub (1973, Section 3, Theorem 1). The affine distance
correction follows directly by moving the closest point or intersection to the origin.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: Classical and solved after fixing the acting
group. An angle alone is not a complete invariant of disjoint affine configurations.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

The notes. Problem 2 asks for the angle between two planes in three-dimensional

the immediately preceding task includes disjoint affine objects.

1987-737.4
A vector and a subspace require magnitude as well as

Class E
Kind; source. problem; P001435
The notes. Problem 4 gives v ∈L, a k-dimensional subspace V, and an inner product

Statement. Under O(L), a pair (v,V) is classified by n, k, ∥PV v∥, ∥PV ⊥v∥. For v ̸= 0,

Every pair of nonnegative component norms is possible when 0 < k < n; if k=0 the

1987-737.5
Finite-dimensional pairs: complete invariants and
their exact range
Class E
Kind; source. problem; P001436
The notes. Problem 5 asks for the angle between subspaces V,W of dimensions k,m
in an n-dimensional inner-product space.
Statement. Let r = min(k, m) and q = max(0, k + m −n). The ordered principal angles
0 ≤θ1 ≤· · · ≤θr ≤π/2, with at least q zero entries, classify ordered pairs under O(n),
or under U(n) in the complex version. Every list satisfying exactly these restrictions
is attainable. The dimensions of the ambient space and of both subspaces are also
part of the classification when they are not fixed.

1987-737.9
Passing from the full orthogonal group to the special
group
Class E
Kind; source. problem; P001436
The notes. After the indefinite variants the sheet asks that all listed problems be
considered for both O(n) and SO(n).
Statement. For a nondegenerate real quadratic space, an O-orbit splits into two
SO-orbits exactly when the stabilizer has no determinant-minus-one element; this
follows because SO has index two. In the Euclidean ordered-pair case, it splits ex-
actly when all four intersections U ∩W, U ∩W ⊥, U ⊥∩W, U ⊥∩W ⊥vanish. Equivalently,
n = 2k = 2m and every principal angle lies strictly between zero and π/2. Otherwise
a reflection in one of those intersections keeps the orbit unsplit. For a degener-
ate form the full preserving group can have arbitrary nonzero determinant on its
radical, so this index-two argument cannot simply be transferred.
Reconstruction. The stabilizer criterion and the degeneracy caveat are supplied;
the latter prevents an incorrect universal two-orbit claim.
Fate. The criterion is an elementary index-two orbit calculation, with the Euclidean
stabilizer read from the principal-angle blocks of Björck and Golub (1973, Sec-
tion 3). The invariant is augmented by a binary orientation choice exactly in the
split case.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: Solved for the stated nondegenerate
groups.
The notation O(n),SO(n) in the degenerate extension needs a definition
and cannot be used to infer an index-two subgroup there.

excluded from this d-angle statement.

The notes. Problem 11 asks for a complete invariant of two Hilbert subspaces and

Statement. For closed M,N, use the four intersection dimensions and the generic

continuous spectrum [0,1] for C but no eigenvectors at those spectral values.

displayed examples.

1987-737.15
Encode operator similarity by four subspaces
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P001437
The notes. The final comment says that a linear operator can be specified by a
quadruple of subspaces.
Statement. For T : E →E, the ordered quadruple E⊕0, 0⊕E, {(x, x)}, {(x, Tx)} in E⊕E
determines T up to similarity. Any isomorphism preserving the first two subspaces
is block diagonal; preservation of the diagonal forces the two blocks to coincide,
and preservation of the last graph is exactly the similarity relation. Conversely any
similarity gives such a configuration isomorphism.
Fate. Gel’fand and Ponomarev (1971, Section 1, Example 1) explicitly gives the
graph construction, with the final two labels interchanged. It is already indexed as
1970-198.3.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: Established exact reduction. The source
is an expository reminder rather than a new representation theorem.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: exact; verification: read
the result; development: none; confidence: high; problem GP-1987-36

1987-738.2
Disjoint additivity does not imply a smooth local
density from continuity alone
Class B
Kind; source. claim; P001438; I. M. Gelfand
The notes. Gelfand says that increments supported in two disjoint places add inde-
pendently, and that this property together with continuity forces a finite-jet integral
of the displayed type.
Statement. On an open subset U ⊂C∞(M), the additivity condition is F(φ + ψ + χ) =
F(φ + ψ) + F(ψ + χ) −F(ψ) when supp φ ∩supp χ = ∅and all arguments belong to
U.
Continuity alone is insufficient: F(u) = u(x0) is continuous and additive, but
its differential is the Dirac distribution, not integration against a smooth density.
A precise characterization additionally assumes Bastiani smoothness, each DFu a
compactly supported smooth density, and smooth dependence of this density on u in
the test-function topology. The conclusion is a finite-jet integral in a neighborhood
of each configuration, with order allowed to depend on that neighborhood.
Reconstruction. The function space, topology, smooth density requirement and lo-
cally bounded jet order are reconstructed from the later theorem; they are not
present in the notebook.
Fate. Brouder et al. (2018, Theorems I.2 and VI.3), first circulated 3 May 2017 and
published in 2018, proves this characterization with the stated regularity hypothe-
ses. Its locally finite-order conclusion is weaker than a single global bound on jet
order.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: The literal continuity-only claim is false.
A precise regular version is proved; no general continuity theorem or global finite-
order theorem is inferred from it.
Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: partial; verifica-
tion: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high; problem GP-1987-

1987-738.4
A hierarchy of transcendence defined by variational
differentiation
Class F
Kind; source. question; P001439; I. M. Gelfand
The notes. Type zero means local; type one means that δF/δu is local; higher types
are recursive. The first eigenvalue is a motivation, and the speaker predicts func-
tionals of all types in characteristic-class theory.
Statement. The notes do not specify how a function-valued differential, a distri-
butional one-form or a higher variation is to belong to the same class as a scalar
functional. Nor do they specify the operator and boundary conditions defining λ1,
the treatment of spectral multiplicities, equivalence modulo constants, or the global
configuration space. The recursive words therefore do not determine a single clas-
sification problem or a theorem that the hierarchy is strict at every level.
Reconstruction. No modern differential-cohomology or spectral-invariant theory is
silently substituted for the proposed hierarchy.
Fate. The named antecedent is identifiable as Gabrielov et al. (1976, Introduction
and Section 1), which studies characteristic-class boundary functionals. That con-
crete paper does not in the sections read formulate the unrestricted recursive hier-
archy asserted here; later local-functional characterizations likewise do not settle
an unspecified notion of transcendence.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: The general hierarchy remains uniden-
tifiable as a definite theorem from this source. This F classification concerns the

never developed.

Statement. On a specified smooth field space, the algebra generated by local scalar

fixed; the residue trace is not confused with the operator trace on a Hilbert space.
Fate. The residue-symbol and variational calculus occurs before the talk in Gel’fand
and Dikii (1976, 1978). The formula is also a direct coefficient check using cyclicity
of the integrated residue; no analytic trace-class assertion is involved.

1987-738.10
The first Gelfand–Dikii bracket
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P001441
The notes. Equations (11) and (13) assert that {F, G}1 = Tr([L, XF ]XG) gives a Lie
bracket on the stated functionals.
Statement. For monic scalar differential operators of fixed positive order over the
differential-polynomial algebra, use the residue gradient of item 1987-738.9. The
map J1,L(X) = [L, X]+ is Hamiltonian, and the displayed pairing is a skew bracket sat-
isfying Jacobi on local functionals. The negative part of [L, XF ] does not contribute
when paired with the negative-order representative XG. The leading coefficient is
fixed; the coefficient of DN−1 is constant along these Hamiltonian vector fields.
Reconstruction. The fixed-order space, quotient by total derivatives and harmless
projection under the trace are made explicit.
Fate. This is established before the talk. Kupershmidt and Wilson (1981, Section 5,
Examples 2–3) states the exact formula and explains its restriction to zero sublead-
ing coefficient; Gel’fand and Dikii (1976, Sections 10–11) supplies an earlier formal
variational Lie-bracket construction.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: The Jacobi assertion is proved in the
specified formal setting; it is not a new open question in this report. Finite products
of actual scalar functionals can be included by the Leibniz rule.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
