1987-738.11
The second bracket, compatibility, and the need for
reduction
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P001441
The notes. Equation (12) gives J2,L(X) = L(XL)+−(LX)+L, and equation (13) asserts
the Lie property. The subsequent examples put the subleading coefficient equal to
zero.
Statement. The pairing {F, G}2 = Tr(J2,L(XF )XG) is Poisson on monic scalar differ-
ential operators with all lower coefficients variable. It is compatible with the first
bracket: every constant linear combination is Poisson. To pass to aN−1 = 0, the
cotangent representative must be corrected by a D−N term so that res[X, L] = 0;
the resulting Hamiltonian vector field is tangent to the constraint. Merely inserting
aN−1 = 0 in the unreduced formula is insufficient.
Reconstruction. Compatibility and the tangency correction are supplied from the
proved formulation. The source sign is retained, and its missing reduction is not
concealed.
Fate. Kupershmidt and Wilson (1981, Section 5, Examples 4–6; Theorems 8.6 and
11.2) gives the bracket with the opposite overall sign, proves the Hamiltonian prop-
erty and explains the reduction. Its bibliography identifies the 1978 Gelfand–Dikii
IPM preprint136 mentioned in the notebook. Khovanova (1986, Sections 1 and 6)
uses precisely the notebook sign.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: Established before March1988. Overall
sign changes preserve the Poisson property, but an omitted reduction changes the
actual bracket and the Virasoro normalization.

1987-738.14

Class E

The notes. The product L = (D + v1) · · · (D + vN) defines the coefficient fields, and the
modes of the vi have independent Heisenberg brackets h nδijδn+m,0.
Statement. Take the vi as independent differential variables with the constant diag-

coefficient of L a differential polynomial in them. The induced bracket on those co-
efficients is the second Gelfand–Dikii bracket, with the common overall scale and
sign fixed by the chosen pairing. Reduction to zero subleading coefficient requires
the corresponding constraint P
i vi = 0 and the reduced current bracket; indepen-

Reconstruction. Formal differential variables, factor order, normalization and the
separate constrained-current reduction are specified.
Fate. Kupershmidt and Wilson (1981, Theorem 11.5, p. 436; Theorem 8.6 for the re-

whereas current Springer metadata gives1980; receipt was February1980 and revi-

before quantizing it.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: Established Poisson realization. It is a

1987-738.16
Nonlinear Poisson relations do not destroy the Lie
property
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P001442
The notes. The notes say that a Lie algebra has become isomorphic to a quadratic
algebra that is not a Lie algebra; Khovanova is named, and an unspecific comparison
with Dynkin follows.
Statement. A Poisson algebra is always a Lie algebra under its bracket, even when
brackets of selected generators are quadratic polynomials rather than linear com-
binations of those generators. Thus the span of the chosen coefficient fields may
fail to be Lie-closed, while the full Poisson algebra still satisfies Jacobi. The Miura
map is a Poisson realization, and the existence of a nonlinear formula does not make
it an isomorphism onto the entire current algebra.
Reconstruction. The meanings of generator span, Poisson algebra and realization
are separated; no specific Dynkin theorem is guessed.
Fate. Khovanova (1986, Section 1, Lemma 1; Section 7) explicitly constructs
Gelfand–Dikii Lie algebras, directly contradicting the literal denial of a Lie struc-
ture. Kupershmidt and Wilson (1981, Theorem 11.5) states the Miura restriction
theorem, not a universal isomorphism.
The Dynkin remark names no definite
algebra or theorem.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: The literal formulation is false. The mean-
ingful distinction is between a linearly closed generating space and nonlinear Pois-
son relations in the larger algebra.
Assessment. literal statement: false as written; publication match: partial; verifi-
cation: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

1987-739.1
Representations of an acyclic quiver
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P001444
The notes. A quiver is called a directed graph without cycles; M(Λ, Ω) is its repre-
sentation category.
Statement. For a finite quiver Q = (Q0, Q1) with no oriented cycles over a field k, let
repk Q consist of finite-dimensional vertex spaces and linear arrow maps. It is an
abelian hereditary category, equivalent to finite-dimensional modules over kQ.
Reconstruction. Finiteness, the coefficient field, and oriented rather than undi-
rected cycles are made explicit.
Fate. This is the framework of Bernstein et al. (1973b, §1); the derived-category
report uses it in Parthasarathy (1988, §1).

1987-739.2
Orientation independence and its graph hypothesis
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P001444
The notes. The lemma says D(Λ, Ω1) ≃D(Λ, Ω2) for different orientations, with reflec-
tions as proof.
Statement. A reflection at a source or sink induces an equivalence of bounded de-
rived categories. Hence orientations joined by such reflections have equivalent cat-
egories; in particular this holds for any two orientations of a finite tree, including
every Dynkin tree.
Reconstruction. The tree or reflection-equivalence hypothesis is essential; the draw-
ing alone does not establish it.
Fate. Parthasarathy (1988, Propositions 1.4 and 1.6) constructs the derived reflec-
tion equivalences from BGP functors. An unrestricted assertion for all acyclic orien-
tations of an arbitrary graph is not supplied by this argument. Derived equivalence
under tilting was already proved in Happel (1987, §1.7, Corollary, p. 344), published
in 1987 after an Ottawa 1984 announcement; applied to a source/sink reflection, it
supplies the pre-talk derived-category formulation.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: derived reflection invariance is estab-
lished; the notebook’ s unrestricted orientation wording should not be used as a
theorem.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: read the result; development: annotated; confidence: high
1987-739.3
Hearts with the same derived category
Class B
Kind; source. problem; P001444-P001445
The notes. Find abelian subcategories A ⊂D with Dℓ(A) = D; the notes say all are
found and characterized by Dynkin graphs.
Statement. Let k be an algebraically closed field and Q a finite acyclic quiver. De-
scribe bounded t-structures on Db(repk Q) whose heart A is a finite-length abelian k-
category with a projective generator and finite-dimensional Hom spaces, and whose
realization functor Db(A) →Db(repk Q) is an equivalence.
Reconstruction. The superscript is read as bounded derived category, and abelian
subcategory is interpreted as a heart. The stated finiteness conditions prevent a
claim about arbitrary embedded abelian categories.
Fate. Parthasarathy (1988, Theorems 3.3, 7.1 and 8.5) gives a criterion for the spec-
ified t-structure data, relates the constructed hearts to iterated tilted algebras, and
proves completeness in the Dynkin case. It was received 15 February 1988, revised
13 May, and published in December 1988 by Parthasarathy, whose work Kapra-
nov reports here; receipt alone does not establish public pre-talk circulation of the
criterion. The Dynkin algebra classification itself was already Happel (1987, Theo-
rem 5.12, p. 358), published in 1987 after an Ottawa 1984 announcement. Thus the
later publication match is the explicit heart-realization approach, not a claim that
the Dynkin classification first appeared in 1988. The notebook blanket sentence is
broader than either precise formulation.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: the identified finite-type/iterated-tilting
classification is established; an unrestricted classification is not asserted.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: medium;

The notes. There is a proof containing an algorithm.
Statement. For a finite bipartite graph, start with a matching and repeatedly aug-
ment along alternating paths. If no augmenting path exists while a left vertex is
unmatched, its alternating reachable sets violate Hall’s condition. Thus the proce-
dure finds a complete left matching under that condition.
Reconstruction. The notes do not name a running time or a particular implementa-
tion; no algorithmic novelty is inferred.
Fate. Hall (1935, proof of Theorem 1) gives a constructive replacement argument.
The augmenting-path formulation is an explicit modern reconstruction of the terse
algorithmic remark.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: established under the stated hypotheses.
Assessment. literal statement: correct; publication match: partial; verification:
read the result; development: none; confidence: high
1987-740.5
A common system of left and right coset
representatives
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P001447
The notes. A finite subgroup has representatives that work simultaneously for its
left and right cosets; the statement is attributed to Birkhoff.
Statement. If H ≤G and G is finite, there is a set meeting each left coset and each
right coset of H once. More generally finite H admits such a transversal in arbitrary
G, by applying the finite equal-block matching argument within each double coset
and choosing these transversals.
Reconstruction. For infinite G, each double coset has equally many left and right
cosets because H is finite; a choice of the finite matchings is used.
Fate. Hall (1935, Theorem 3 and its equal-size corollary, pp. 29–30) supplies the
partition theorem; its application to cosets is immediate. Hall credits the equal-
block result to Konig, so the notebook’s attribution is not independently confirmed.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: established under the stated hypotheses.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high

I. M. Gelfand, 1988-03-23: Famous problems as
sources of mathematical theories
record 741; source pages P001447.
Gelfand lists the parallel postulate, classical construction problems, Fermat’s prob-
lem, the four-colour problem, and the Riemann hypothesis. These are historical and
methodological remarks, not claims of new proofs. Faltings appears in the margin;
the finiteness of rational points must not be confused with Fermat’s theorem.
1987-741.1
The parallel postulate and non-Euclidean models
Class E
Kind; source. remark; P001447
The notes. The parallel axiom is listed as a seemingly fruitless problem that pro-
duced non-Euclidean geometry.
Statement. Models of hyperbolic geometry satisfy the other standard inci-
dence/congruence axioms but not Euclid’s parallel axiom; relative to the con-
sistency of the background geometry, the axiom cannot be deduced from those
remaining axioms.
Reconstruction. The formal independence statement is relative to an axiom system
and consistency assumption, neither specified in the remark.
Fate. Beltrami (1869) is the opened 1869 French publication of Beltrami’s 1868
interpretation. This supports the mathematical endpoint, not an exhaustive causal
history.

1987-741.2
Classical construction impossibilities
Class E
Kind; source. remark; P001447
The notes. Angle trisection and circle quadrature are linked with the development
of Galois theory.
Statement. With an unmarked straightedge and compass, an arbitrary given angle
cannot be trisected, and a square of the same area as a given circle cannot be
constructed. The first follows from a cubic obstruction, for example trisection of
60◦; the second follows from the transcendence of π.
Reconstruction. The allowed instruments and the general rather than every-angle
quantifier are supplied.
Fate. Wantzel (1837, §1 and the applications) treats algebraic constructibility and
trisection. The two impossibilities have different proofs; quadrature is not a conse-
quence solely of finite Galois theory. The opened publisher record verifies Linde-
mann (1882); its original proof was not read in this run.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: established under the stated hypotheses.

Statement. For every integer n > 2, there are no positive integers a, b, c with an+bn =

Exceptional bundles and ordered exceptional

Class E
Kind; source. definition; P001448
The notes. For bundles E1, E2, E3 on P2, the notes impose scalar endomorphisms,

Statement. An object E ∈Db(Coh X) is exceptional when Hom(E, E) = k and
Ext
p(E, E) = 0 for p ̸= 0. An ordered exceptional collection satisfies Ext
p(Ej, Ei) = 0
for every p and j > i. Here the objects are algebraic vector bundles on P2.

Fate. Rudakov (1988a, §1) states the bundle framework, already developed by the

Exceptional ranks satisfy the Markov equation
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P001448
The notes. The ranks x1, x2, x3 of the exceptional triple satisfy x2
1 + x2
2 + x2
3 = 3x1x2x3.
Statement. For a full exceptional triple of vector bundles on P2, its positive ranks

Reconstruction. Fullness and positive ranks are stated explicitly; this is not a rank

1987-743.1
Finite, tame, and wild representation problems
Class E
Kind; source. remark; P001448; I. M. Gelfand
The notes. Gelfand distinguishes no parameters, one parameter, and at least two
parameters, comparing the last with classifying pairs of matrices.
Statement. For a fixed finite-dimensional algebra over an algebraically closed
field, finite representation type means finitely many indecomposable isomorphism
classes; tame type permits finitely many one-parameter families in each dimension,
with finitely many exceptions; wildness means that the problem of modules over
the free algebra in two generators embeds in the representation problem. A raw
count of parameters does not define these alternatives.
Reconstruction. The standard representation-theoretic meanings replace the infor-
mal parameter count.
Fate. For quivers, the finite/Dynkin and extended-Dynkin examples already occur in
1972-277.8 and 1972-278.9. Bernstein et al. (1973b) supplies the finite-type setting.
The remark is not a proof of a general dichotomy theorem.

1987-743.2
An open orbit is not automatic in a wild problem
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P001448; I. M. Gelfand
The notes. Gelfand says an unsolvable classification always contains a solvable part,
adding an open orbit.
Statement. Existence of a dense open orbit is a separate geometric condition. In
simultaneous conjugation of pairs of n × n complex matrices, the invariant tr(A)


CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1987–88
92
varies, so no orbit can be dense in the full affine space of pairs. This already refutes
an unconditional open-orbit assertion.
Reconstruction. [proved here] The text is read literally as claiming an open orbit in
the original representation space; a more restricted stratum could behave differ-
ently.
Fate. This is an elementary editorial check: a polynomial invariant is constant on
an orbit and its closure. No external classification theorem is needed. The BGP
finite-type examples give context only.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: false without a specified stratum or addi-
tional hypotheses.

1987-743.3
Self-Ext vanishing and infinitesimal rigidity
Class E
Kind; source. claim; P001449
The notes. Ext1(E, E) = 0 is interpreted as rigidity of a vector bundle.
Statement. For a vector bundle on a proper scheme over a field, first-order deforma-

Reconstruction. The meaning of rigidity is restricted to infinitesimal deformations,
with the usual fixed base and deformation functor.
Fate. The deformation-free exceptional-bundle setting is explicit in Rudakov (1988a,

1.31
A. A. Beilinson, 1988-03-23: Constructible sheaves,
singular support, and microlocal Morse groups
record 744; source pages P001449-P001452.
The report, attributed to work of S. Gelfand, MacPherson and Vilonen, proceeds
from constructibility to singular support, Lagrangian geometry and a Morse charac-
terization of perversity. Several schematic assertions need the distinction between
one test function and a conic neighbourhood, local and global finiteness, and Maslov-
normalized Morse data. The zero section cannot simply be added over points outside
the support.

1987-744.7
Morse transversality to a singular support
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P001451
The notes. The graph of df must meet Y only at smooth points and transversely;
examples are the zero section and a smooth conormal.
Statement. For a holomorphic function f and conic Lagrangian Y , a microlocal
Morse point is a transverse intersection of Γdf with Ysm, away from the singular part
of Y . For Y = T ∗
XX this is an ordinary nondegenerate critical point; for Y = T ∗
ZX
with smooth Z, it is a nondegenerate critical point of f|Z.

Fate. This is the test-function construction used in Gelfand et al. (2005, Introduction

1987-744.8
Perversity detected by Morse groups
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P001452
The notes. All Morse relative hypercohomology groups must occur in degree zero;
a parenthetical remark instead says first degree.
Statement. With the convention that kX[dimC X] is perverse,
a constructible
bounded complex is perverse exactly when its normalized local Morse groups are

vanishing-cycle cone, and cannot coexist literally with degree zero without specify-

Basic perverse sheaves and the closed-embedding

Kind; source. construction; P001452
The notes. Examples are a skyscraper, CX[n], and CZ[dim Z] extended by zero from

1.32
D. B. Fuchs, 1988-04-11: Reducibility and
discharging in the four-colour theorem
record 745; source pages P001453-P001457.
Fuchs explains the Appel–Haken strategy through minimal counterexamples, re-

1987-745.1
Configurations, reducibility, and unavoidability
Class E
Kind; source. definition; P001453
The notes. A configuration is a triangulated disc; an unavoidable collection occurs
in every triangulation, and a reducible member cannot occur in a smallest coun-
terexample.
Statement. In a finite simple plane triangulation, a configuration specifies a finite
near-triangulation together with the required degrees of its internal vertices and a
boundary ring. Reducibility means that an occurrence can be replaced by a smaller
graph whose four-colouring can be lifted, possibly using Kempe changes. An un-
avoidable set is one occurring in every triangulation of the relevant reduced class.
Reconstruction. Degree and boundary data and the relevant class of minimal coun-
terexamples are restored.
Fate. Gonthier (2005, §3) states the established proof structure, already present in
the 1977 Appel–Haken proof. An arbitrary disc triangulation without embedding
and degree data is not the complete configuration specification.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: established under the stated hypotheses.

exact; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high
1987-745.2
An unavoidable reducible family proves
four-colourability
Class E
Kind; source. method; P001453
The notes. The main idea is to combine the two definitions and induct on the number
of vertices.
Statement. If every minimal non-four-colourable plane triangulation contains a
member of a fixed family, and every member is reducible, no minimal counterex-
ample exists. After deleting duplicate edges, a finite loopless planar graph with
at least three vertices extends to a simple plane triangulation; smaller graphs are
immediate. Restricting a four-colouring yields the theorem.
Fate. The proof scheme was realized in Appel et al. (1977); Gonthier (2005, §3, parts
I–II) states its formalized version.

1987-745.3
The cosine addition identity as a spherical-function

Class E
Kind; source. remark; P001454; I. M. Gelfand
The notes. Gelfand says his spherical-function theory arose from cos(α+β)+cos(α−

1987-745.4
Degree-four vertices are reducible by a Kempe
argument
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P001454
The notes. For four neighbours with all four colours, either the 1–3 Kempe path is
absent, or its planar separation prevents a 2–4 path; a colour swap frees a colour.
Statement. In a plane triangulation, a vertex of degree four cannot belong to a
minimal non-four-colourable graph. Remove it and colour the smaller graph. If the
four cyclic neighbours use four different colours, disjoint alternating 1–3 and 2–4
connecting paths cannot both exist in the punctured plane; swap on a component
of one colour pair and extend the colouring.
Reconstruction. The cyclic order and planarity are the essential topological hy-
potheses.
Fate. Gonthier (2005, §3, the square case) records this classical Kempe reduction.
The valid degree-four argument does not validate Kempe’s failed degree-five argu-
ment.

1987-745.5
Boundary colourings and Kempe closure
Class E
Kind; source. method; P001455
The notes. Some boundary colourings extend into a configuration, others do not; a
computer searches for recolourings as in the previous example.
Statement. For a fixed finite ring configuration, enumerate proper four-colourings
of the ring and the subset extendible through the interior. Reducibility tests com-
bine this finite data with the permitted noncrossing Kempe-chain pairing informa-

bility do not establish reducibility.
Reconstruction. The exterior noncrossing compatibility, not spelled out in the
sketch, is essential.
Fate. Gonthier (2005, §5.2) formalizes the Kempe-chain and reducibility calculation.
The 1977 proof already used finite reducibility algorithms; the later proof verifies
a specific implementation.

1987-745.9
Discharging preserves the total charge
Class E
Kind; source. method; P001456-P001457
The notes. Assign charge 60(6−i) to degree i, redistribute 30 along specified edges,
and seek a contradiction by making every final charge nonpositive.
Statement. The initial charge sum is 720. Any pairwise transfer conserves it. There-
fore, if absence of a finite configuration family forces every final charge to be non-
positive under a fully specified set of transfer rules, that family is unavoidable. The
single sample rule in the notes does not by itself prove the required universal im-
plication.
Reconstruction. Conservation is exact; completeness of the discharge rules is not
reconstructed from the incomplete examples.
Fate. Gonthier (2005, §3, discharging steps 1–2) gives the precise proof pattern.
Appel–Haken’s completed rule system is a much larger object than this board
sketch.
For the manuscript computer-time question, the contemporary digest
Bernhart (1977, abstract) reports 1200 hours for nearly 1900 configurations.

1987-746.4
Reconstruct perverse sheaves from microlocal Morse
data
Class C
Kind; source. problem; P001458-P001459
The notes. The proposed third method is a microlocal description by vanishing cy-
cles; the report says the work solves half of the problem.
Statement. Let X be a complex manifold and Λ ⊂T ∗X a specified closed complex
analytic conic Lagrangian. With coefficients in C, describe the category of perverse
sheaves with singular support in Λ by local Morse data on the smooth pieces, glu-
ing at codimension-one loci, and explicit compatibility conditions at singular loci. A
complete answer must characterize the essential image, not only embed the cate-
gory faithfully.
Reconstruction. The fixed-support categorical formulation and essential-image dis-
tinction make the informal reconstruction question precise; no arbitrary missing
codimension-two formula is invented.
Fate. Gelfand et al. (2005, Theorem 5.1 and Theorem 6.4), first circulated on arXiv
in September 2005, proves an equivalence away from the codimension-two locus
and a global fully faithful embedding in melded Morse data. Kashiwara and Vilonen
(2014, Theorem 1.1), announced in 2010 with a full preprint in 2012 and publica-
tion in 2014, proves unique extension across analytic subsets of codimension at
least three in the Lagrangian support. The exact remaining task is formulated as
Problem 9.3 of that paper (published p. 617): describe the conditions imposed by
the codimension-two locus on the essential image. The extension theorem does not
erase these conditions. Guminov (2025, Introduction) confirms that explicit cate-
gory descriptions remain available only for restricted families.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: partially developed over complex coeffi-
cients in the explicit microlocal-data sense stated here. Kashiwara–Vilonen Problem
9.3 explicitly isolates the codimension-two essential-image question. The consulted
later primary literature does not settle that general geometric description; this is a
bounded search conclusion, not a claim that no categorical reconstruction theorem
of any kind exists.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: medium;
problem GP-1987-46
Worth developing. A fixed singular support with concrete codimension-two links
would make a useful bounded project, but the notebook supplies no such geometry
to calculate here.

1987-747.1
The finite Morse index of a geodesic
Class E
Kind; source. theorem; P001461
The notes. The energy functional on the path space has finite negative index, equal
to the number of conjugate points along the geodesic.
Statement. For a geodesic segment in a smooth Riemannian manifold with fixed
endpoints, the Hessian of energy has finite Morse index. It equals the sum of the
multiplicities of conjugate points to the initial endpoint in the open segment; con-
jugacy at the final endpoint contributes to nullity, not to the negative index.
Reconstruction. Fixed endpoints and the endpoint/nullity convention are supplied.
Fate. Duistermaat (1974, §§1–3) formulates the index in variational and Lagrangian
terms. Boundary conditions and multiplicities, omitted in the board sentence, de-
termine the exact result.

1987-747.2
Relative grading for strongly indefinite functionals
Class B
Kind; source. method; P001461
The notes. Critical points may have infinitely many positive and negative directions,
but the numbers of negative directions are said to differ by a finite number; homol-
ogy is counted from infinity over two.
Statement. For a continuous path of self-adjoint Fredholm Hessians with invertible
ends and appropriate operator-domain control, spectral flow supplies a finite rela-
tive index. Infinite positive and negative indices alone do not imply that an index
difference exists. A Floer complex additionally requires compactness, transversal-
ity, coherent orientation and control of broken trajectories.
Reconstruction. The Fredholm and analytic hypotheses replace subtraction of two
infinite cardinalities.
Fate. Atiyah (1988, pp. 286–294) explains the gauge-theoretic Morse picture, circu-
lated as a 1987 lecture/preprint. Floer (1988), received 3 May and published June
1988, establishes the associated homology-sphere invariant. This is exposition of a
programme under development, not a general theorem for arbitrary functionals.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: relative-index Floer theories are estab-
lished under specific analytic hypotheses; the unrestricted formulation is invalid.
Assessment. literal statement: correct with missing hypotheses; publication match:
partial; verification: read the result; development: none; confidence: high
1987-747.3
Relation with Donaldson invariants
Class E
Kind; source. remark; P001461
The notes. The striking claim is that these invariants are related to Donaldson in-
variants of four-manifolds; gauge fields and flat connections are drawn.
Statement. The relevant functional on three-dimensional connections is Chern–
Simons: its critical points are flat connections and its cylinder gradient equation
is the anti-self-dual equation in four dimensions. Donaldson theory can therefore
interact with the boundary Floer groups through cutting and gluing.
Reconstruction. The functional is reconstructed as Chern–Simons from the named
source and later continuation; Yang–Mills criticality alone would be insufficient.
Fate. Atiyah (1988, §§2–5) is the named source; record 751 supplies the detailed
seminar continuation. The ordinary Yang–Mills energy on a three-manifold can have
nonflat critical points, so the functional in the sketch must not be identified with it
without correction.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: established under the stated hypotheses.

N. Yu. Reshetikhin, 1988-04-18: Quantum groups,

record 749; source pages P001461-P001467.
Reshetikhin gives the rank-one quantum enveloping algebra, coproduct and rep-

congress report. Reshetikhin’s LOMI preprints E-4-87 and E-17-87 closely match the
talk, but the scanned editions bear a 1988 imprint; a 1987 series number and rec-

normalization formulas in the notes need correction; generic deformation parameters
and framing conventions are indispensable. The assertion that the rank-one invariants
suffice has no stated separation criterion.

1987-749.2
The symmetric coproduct and counit
Class E

The notes. ∆H = H ⊗1 + 1 ⊗H, ∆X± = X± ⊗qH/4 + q−H/4 ⊗X±; this is an algebra

H1(S2) = 0.

1987-749.12
Graphical tensor calculus, duality, and ribbon
structure
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P001464-P001466
The notes. Composition joins diagrams; straight strands denote identities, crossings
R and its inverse, and cups/caps have signed q-weight formulas.
Statement. In a rigid braided tensor category, composition and tensor product are
represented by stacking and juxtaposition. Evaluation and coevaluation must sat-
isfy the two zigzag identities; a ribbon twist supplies compatibility with framed
isotopy. Explicit cup/cap coefficients depend on chosen identifications with duals
and bases, particularly for half-integer spins.

quantum-group graphical programme. The two inconsistent-looking q-powers in
the notes cannot be repaired uniquely without those basis choices. The scanned
LOMI editions carry a 1988 imprint despite their 1987 series identifiers; an exact

1987-749.15
What does sufficiency of the SL2 invariants mean?
Class F
Kind; source. conjecture; P001467
The notes. There is a conjecture that the SL2-invariants suffice; no object or equiv-
alence relation follows the verb.
Statement. If interpreted as separation of all oriented knot types by all colours
of the Jones polynomial, the assertion is false. But the notes could instead mean
recovery of another family of quantum invariants, or another weaker property; the
exact conjecture is not recoverable.
Reconstruction. The separation interpretation is deliberately conditional and is not
entered as the original conjecture in the problem index.
Fate. Stoimenow and Tanaka (2008, Theorem 1.1) constructs infinitely many pairs
of hyperbolic knots with equal coloured Jones polynomials that are not mutants. The
paper records a first manuscript in December 2005 and arXiv circulation in 2006;
the opened version is from 2008. This refutes the explicit separation interpretation,
not every possible reading of “suffice.
Present status. As of 11 November 2026: the exact statement is unidentified; the
most natural complete-knot-invariant interpretation has published counterexam-
ples.

1.37
A. G. Ushveridze, 1988-04-25: Riccati equations and
quasi-exact solvability
record 750; source pages P001468-P001472.
The talk constructs finite families of Riccati solutions from poles satisfying Bethe-
type equations, with rational, trigonometric and elliptic addition laws. It discusses
electrostatics, common eigenspaces and conversion to Schrodinger equations. The
author’s 1989 review is a close primary source. Two addition-law constants and the
root equations in the manuscript require explicit repair.

1987-750.5
Logarithmic derivatives linearize the Riccati
equation
Class E
Kind; source. method; P001469-P001470
The notes. Putting y = f′/f is said to give a Schrodinger equation; the later page
writes f′′ + 2Bf′ + Cf = 0.
Statement. On an interval where f ̸= 0, the substitution is equivalent to f′′ + 2Bf′ +
Cf = 0. Conversely, any Riccati solution gives a local nonzero f by exponentiating
its integral. A gauge and coordinate change are still needed to obtain Schrodinger
normal form; the first substitution alone leaves a first derivative.
Reconstruction. [computed here] The local nonvanishing condition and distinction
between a linear ODE and Schrodinger normal form are explicit.
Fate. Ushveridze (1989, §§2–3) gives the analytic normal-form method. The equiv-
alence follows directly from y′ + y2 = f′′/f.

Statement. On a common invariant domain, let V = ker(H1 −c1) ∩ker(H2 −c2) and

Then H|V = H0|V.
In particular every known H0-eigenvector
in V remains an H-eigenvector; no commutation of the Lj with Hi is needed.
A

The notes. The product A = Q
α Ξ(λ −aα), Ξ′/Ξ = ∆, is said to put all root information

Two fixed positive charges and Jacobi polynomials
Class A
Kind; source. theorem; P001471
The notes. For N = 2, b1, b2 > 0, repulsion traps M particles between a1 < a2, giving
a solution with M nodes for every M and an exactly solvable model.
Statement. Let a1 < a2 be real, b1, b2 > 0, and M ≥0 an integer. On the chamber
a1 < λ1 < · · · < λM < a2, the negative-log energy is strictly convex and tends to infinity
at its boundary, hence has a unique minimizer. The monic polynomial fM = Q
i(λ−λi)
satisfies Af′′

B(λ) = b1/(λ −a1) + b2/(λ −a2); for M=0 the configuration is empty and f0 = 1. These

Exterior intervals and finitely many admissible

Class A
Kind; source. claim; P001471-P001472
The notes. For M particles to the right of the second fixed charge, the stability
condition is written b1 + b2 + M < 0; failure for large M yields only finitely many
levels.
Statement. With b2 > 0, the displayed inequality is a sufficient confining condition
for the negative-log energy on the exterior chamber, but it is not generally the sharp
threshold. Already for M=1, a1 = 0, a2 = 1, b1 = −1, b2 = 1/2, U = log λ −1

has a strict minimum at λ = 2, although b1 + b2 + M = 1/2. Finiteness of normalizable

Reconstruction. [computed here] The counterexample only rejects necessity of the
displayed condition; it does not reject that condition as a sufficient bound.
Fate. Ushveridze (1989, §5, equations (62)–(63) and the following comparison,

1987-751.1
The finite-dimensional Morse complex
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P001472-P001473
The notes. For a compact manifold and generic metric, trajectories from x1 to x2
have dimension ind(x1) −ind(x2) −1, and signed index-one trajectories define the
differential. The vector field is written + grad f.
Statement. For a Morse function on a closed manifold and a Morse–Smale metric,
use the negative gradient. The unparametrized trajectory space from p to q has
dimension ind p−ind q−1. Choose orientations of unstable manifolds; signed counts
for index difference one define d : Ci →Ci−1, with d2 = 0, and its homology is the
ordinary homology. Mod-two coefficients avoid the orientation choices.
Reconstruction. Closedness, Morse–Smale transversality, quotient by time transla-
tion and coherent orientation choices are stated explicitly.
Fate. Atiyah (1988, §3, pp. 287–288) recalls this construction after Witten (1982,
§2). The notebook combines positive-gradient notation with the negative-gradient
index convention.

1987-751.2
The irreducible gauge quotient
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P001473
The notes. The main manifold is X = C/G, irreducible Hermitian connections on the
trivial rank-two bundle, with G = SU(2)M.
Statement. Let M be a closed connected oriented smooth three-manifold. On the
trivial SU(2)-bundle, connections form A = d + Ω1(M; su2). An irreducible connection
has stabilizer the centre {±1}; after Sobolev completion, the effective gauge group
G/{±1} acts freely and the quotient has local Coulomb slices. Reducible points are
excluded from this manifold assertion.
Reconstruction. Orientation, completion and the ineffective central stabilizer are
restored.
Fate. Atiyah (1988, §2, pp. 286–287) gives precisely the irreducible quotient used
in the Casson/Floer picture.
The trivial-bundle condition is automatic for SU(2)-
bundles over a three-dimensional CW complex, but is retained from the notes.

The notes. The Hessian is
R

1987-751.6
Spectral flow and the relative grading modulo eight
Class B
Kind; source. construction; P001475
The notes. Index difference is called the number of points where a zero mode ap-
pears, then spectral asymmetry; it is said to be well defined modulo eight.
Statement. For a path of self-adjoint Fredholm gauge-fixed Hessians with nonde-
generate ends, spectral flow is the signed crossing count with multiplicity (or its
perturbation-invariant definition), not the unsigned number of zero events. In the
SU(2) instanton theory of an integral homology sphere, changing a path by a gener-
ating gauge loop changes this index by eight; hence the relative grading descends
modulo eight.
Reconstruction. Signs, multiplicities, gauge fixing and the homology-sphere gauge-
theory setup are required for the stated eight-periodicity.
Fate. Atiyah (1988, §4, p. 289) gives the spectral-flow calculation and its index-
theorem origin. Floer (1988) establishes the resulting mod-eight graded homology,
received 3 May and published June 1988; only its publisher abstract, not its full
proof, was read here.

1987-751.8
The closed action one-form and Maslov relative index
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P001476-P001477
The notes. On paths from L1 to L2, define νγ(ξ) =
R 1
0 ω(˙γ, ξ)dt; zeros are intersections,
and a relative index is obtained from Maslov theory.
Statement. Let (N, ω) be symplectic and L1, L2 Lagrangian. The action one-form on a
suitable Sobolev path space is closed because its exterior derivative is the endpoint
difference of ω, which vanishes on the Lagrangians. Its zeros are constant paths in
L1 ∩L2. Transverse intersections give nondegenerate Fredholm Hessians; relative
grading uses the Maslov index, with ambiguity determined by the relevant loop
classes. Exactness or a covering is needed for a single-valued action.
Reconstruction. Lagrangian boundary conditions, Sobolev/Fredholm setup and the
action-period ambiguity are explicit; existence of a full Floer homology needs fur-
ther compactness hypotheses.
Fate. Atiyah (1988, §5, pp. 292–293) describes the symplectic Floer construction
and cites Floer’s 1987 preprints. The index formalism has earlier variational back-
ground in Duistermaat (1974, §2).

1987-751.9
Heegaard splitting as a Lagrangian
representation-space intersection
Class E
Kind; source. construction; P001477
The notes. A three-manifold is cut into two pieces along a surface; fundamental
groups and their representations give the finite-dimensional counterpart of the con-
nection construction.
Statement. For a genus-g Heegaard splitting M = W1 ∪Σ W2, each handlebody group
is free of rank g and van Kampen presents π1M as their amalgam over π1Σ. On the
smooth irreducible locus, the SU(2) character variety of Σ has its cup-product sym-
plectic form. The handlebody representation loci are Lagrangian where smooth,
and their intersection represents flat connections on M. Reducibles, singularities
and transversality cannot be ignored.
Reconstruction. The character-variety smooth locus and Lagrangian hypotheses are
supplied rather than claiming that every full representation quotient is a smooth
symplectic manifold.
Fate. Atiyah (1988, §5, pp. 290–293) is the direct publication match. It discusses
the conjectural relation of the two Floer constructions, but the notebook does not
explicitly formulate that homology-isomorphism conjecture, so none is added to this
year’s problem index.

Class B
Kind; source. method; P001477
The notes. Four-dimensional Yang–Mills equations and cutting a four-manifold into

Statement. On M × R, an anti-self-dual connection in temporal gauge satisfies

1.39
Developments
1.39.1
Joint spectra of stochastic matrices: a bounded development
of GP-1987-21
The source question and its scope.
In item 1987-728.4, Gelfand asks for the набор
соб. значений в Cn and emphasizes that projection onto one coordinate may conceal
its geometry. The scan of P001418 supports a question about all eigenvalues of a real
nonnegative row-stochastic matrix. It does not impose symmetry, double stochastic-
ity, irreducibility, a specified zero pattern, or a prescribed Jordan form. Those are
different problems. The strongest precise formulation supported by the page is to
describe, for each positive integer n, the permutation-invariant set of lists for which
there exists a matrix P satisfying
Σn =

(λ1, . . . , λn) ∈Cn : det(tI −P) = Qn
j=1(t −λj),
P ∈Rn×n,
Pij ≥0,
P1 = 1

.
Multiplicities are retained. This is a reconstruction of a shorthand question, not a
conjectured theorem attributed to the speaker.
State of the art and the bounded contribution.
The order-three characterization
predates the seminar: Loewy and London (1978) appeared in 1978; its attribution
and explicit alternatives are recorded in Ran and Teng (2024, p. 506).
The latter
paper additionally classifies prescribed order-three zero patterns and appeared in
July 2024 (received May 2023).
Johnson and Paparella (2025, Theorems 3.9–3.12
and Sections 4–5, 8) establish geometric properties of joint stochastic spectra and
polyhedral families from fixed diagonalizing matrices; their preprint first circulated
on 12 September 2024 and the journal paper appeared in 2025. Their nearly complete


CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1987–88
121
geometric picture in order four does not solve all orders. As of 11 November 2026,
the sources checked provide no general structural solution in orders at least five. The
distinction between diagonalizable and unrestricted realization is substantive; Cronin
and Laffey (2018, Section 2 and Proposition 1) first circulated in January 2017 and
appeared in July 2018.
For a fixed n, polynomial feasibility already makes membership decidable for alge-
braic input. Thus open does not mean that there is no finite decision procedure. What
remains is an explicit, usable description of the joint region, its boundary and realiz-
ing matrices. The contribution here is a self-contained, checked low-dimensional atlas
and precise obstructions to extrapolating necessary tests. None of the results below
is claimed to be new.
Proposition 1.1 (Geometry and a finite formulation [proved here]). For every fixed n,
Σn is compact and semialgebraic in R2n. It is invariant under coordinate permutations
and complex conjugation, and star-shaped at (1, . . . , 1). The set
bΣn = {(λ2, . . . , λn) : (1, λ2, . . . , λn) ∈Σn}
is star-shaped at the origin.
Proof. The stochastic matrices form a compact product of n probability simplices. Ev-
ery eigenvalue has modulus at most one: if Pv = λv and |vi| = maxj |vj| > 0, then |λvi| ≤
P
j Pij|vj| ≤|vi|. For a convergent sequence of lists in Σn, choose corresponding ma-
trices and a convergent subsequence of these matrices. Continuity of characteristic-
polynomial coefficients shows that the limiting matrix realizes the limiting list. Thus
Σn is closed and bounded, without requiring a continuously chosen ordering of eigen-
values.
Writing λj = xj + iyj, equality of coefficients in det(tI −P) = Q
j(t −xj −iyj) is a finite
system of real polynomial equalities. Append Pij ≥0 and P
j Pij = 1. Projecting onto
the x, y coordinates and applying real quantifier elimination gives semialgebraicity
and a decision algorithm for effectively encoded algebraic input.
The background
theorem is the standard one treated in Basu et al. (2006, Chapters 2, 13–14); we do
not reprove that theorem or claim an efficient complexity bound.
Permutation invariance follows from the characteristic polynomial; conjugation in-
variance follows from its real coefficients. For 0 ≤s ≤1, (1 −s)I + sP is stochastic with
eigenvalues 1 −s + sλj, proving the first star-shapedness assertion. Put J = 11T/n and
Q = sP +(1−s)J. On the invariant line spanned by 1, Q is the identity. On the quotient
by that line, it induces s times the map induced by P, because J has image in the line.
In a basis beginning with 1, its characteristic polynomial is therefore (t−1) Qn
j=2(t−sλj).
This also covers repeated eigenvalues and nondiagonalizable matrices.

Example 1.4 (Two insufficient tests [proved here]). Each of −3/4 and 1 occurs in
a stochastic three-state spectrum: use C(−3/4) ⊕(1). Yet {1, −3/4, −3/4} cannot be a
joint spectrum because its trace is −1/2. A product of marginal regions is therefore
too large. Even the modulus, conjugation and all nonnegative power-sum tests can
be insufficient.
The list {1, i/
√

√

Proposition 1.5 (An order-five obstruction surviving all JLL tests [proved here]). The
list Λ = {1, 1, −2/3, −2/3, −2/3} satisfies every power-sum and JLL inequality for n =
5, together with the Perron and conjugation conditions.
It is nevertheless not the
spectrum of any nonnegative 5 × 5 matrix, and hence is not in Σ5.
Proof. Put q = 2/3, so sk = 2 + 3(−q)k. For even k, apply the power-mean inequality to
1, 1, qk, qk, qk, obtaining sm
k ≤5m−1skm. For odd k, 0 ≤sk ≤2, since qk ≤2/3. If m is odd,
skm ≥sk, so sm
k ≤2m−1sk ≤5m−1skm. If m is even, skm ≥2, so sm
k ≤2m ≤5m−1skm. This
proves all inequalities for every positive k, m.
Suppose a nonnegative matrix realizes the list. Put it in Frobenius block-triangular
form, with irreducible nonnegative diagonal blocks, including one-by-one blocks.
Each block has its nonnegative spectral radius as an eigenvalue. The only nonnega-
tive member of Λ is 1, so each block must contain a copy of 1. The Perron eigenvalue
of an irreducible block is simple; there must therefore be exactly two blocks, each
using one copy. One block receives at least two of the three negative eigenvalues.
Its trace is at most 1 −4/3 = −1/3, impossible for a nonnegative block.
This is the classical list recorded in Cronin and Laffey (2018, Section 2). The all-k, m
check and block proof are supplied to make the obstruction independently verifiable,
without claiming novelty.
Independent verification [computed here].
The script code/verify_development.
py checks the characteristic polynomials symbolically and then checks the construc-
tions using a separate exact three-by-three determinant formula on rational grids. It
also tests independently generated stochastic matrices against the inequalities and
checks the order-five example with exact fractions over a finite exponent range. The
finite tests are diagnostic; the universally quantified claims are proved above. The
output is logs/development_checks.json. An independent derivation of the triangular
bound uses tr P 2 = P
i P 2
ii + 2 P
i<j PijPji, followed by Cauchy–Schwarz. The Fourier
eigenvector proof checks the converse separately from the characteristic-polynomial
calculation. Both checks agree, including boundary cases and reducible real-pair re-
alizations.
What remains and what another run could accomplish.
The bottleneck is joint
compatibility: one nonnegative matrix must realize all roots.
Reducibility and re-
peated Perron roots constrain how roots can be grouped. Projection to one eigen-
value, all power-sum inequalities, or one fixed diagonalizing basis loses information.
The calculation does not resolve the general n ≥5 region. There is no evidence that
this question was abandoned: the checked 2024–2025 papers show continued work.
A bounded next run could construct an exact atlas for one specified family of four-
state zero patterns, compare it with the known order-four characterization and the
constructions in Johnson and Paparella (2025, Section 8), and certify every boundary
piece by a realizing matrix or polynomial obstruction. That would provide a reusable
companion/website contribution.
Its novelty would have to be established by that
comparison.

1.39.2
A Poisson-pencil repair for the Neumann calculation
This annotation of item 1987-716.6 records a failure found by the independent check.
With ε123 = 1, the second printed table on P001391 has {Mi, Mj} = εijkakMk, {Mi, pj} =
εijkakpk, and {pi, pj} = εijkMk. Its cyclic Jacobi expression at (M1, p1, p2) is (a3 −a2)M2,
so it is not a Poisson bracket for general diagonal A. Reversing time cannot repair
this defect. The scan confirms the printed coefficients. The following is a consistent
algebraic emendation, not a claim to recover a uniquely intended historical formula.
Proposition 1.6 (A compatible repaired bracket [proved here]). For arbitrary real
a1, a2, a3, the table
{Mi, Mj}2 = εijkakMk,
{Mi, pj}2 = εijkajpk,
{pi, pj}2 = −εijkMk
defines a linear Poisson bracket.
It is compatible with the standard e(3)∗bracket
{Mi, Mj}1 = εijkMk, {Mi, pj}1 = εijkpk, {pi, pj}1 = 0. With F = p2/2, the repaired bracket
gives ˙p = M × p,
˙M = (Ap) × p, exactly the first bracket’s vector field for H = (M2 +
pTAp)/2.
Proof. Let Eij denote the skew matrix with entry 1 in position (i, j), entry −1 in position
(j, i), and all other entries zero.
For any diagonal 4 × 4 matrix D, the product X ∗
Y = XDY is associative. Its commutator [X, Y ]D = XDY −Y DX is consequently a Lie
bracket. Skew matrices are closed under this bracket since DT = D. Choose the basis
m1 = −E23,
m2 = −E31,
m3 = −E12,
qi = Ei4
(1 ≤i ≤3).
For D = diag(a1, a2, a3, −1), direct matrix multiplication gives precisely the three dis-
played structure-constant formulas. The associated Lie–Poisson bracket on the dual
therefore satisfies Jacobi, including when D is singular. For D0 = diag(1, 1, 1, 0), the
same basis gives the first bracket. Every linear combination is again the commuta-
tor for the corresponding linear combination of D, D0, proving compatibility. Finally,
contracting the repaired table with ∂F/∂pj = pj gives the two stated vector fields. Con-
tracting the first table with the derivatives of H gives the same fields, independently
checking their signs.
Second check [computed here].
The coordinate computation in the early-record
verification script tests every coordinate Jacobi identity for the repaired bracket and
the full pencil, and checks equality of the Hamiltonian fields. The associative-matrix
construction above is an independent proof of those identities. Its agreement with the
symbolic output confirms the emendation; the failed original table is retained in that
output.
1.39.3
The complete attainable range in the tensor-angle exercise
This is an elementary annotation of item 1987-737.11 and GP-1987-29, classified E;
no priority claim is made. The preamble on P001435 explicitly asks which values the
invariants in all the exercises can take. On P001436, the tensor exercise specifies a
Euclidean space L and compares a ⊗L with L ⊗b in L⊗3. We reconstruct the finite-
dimensional setting of that part of the sheet, require a, b ̸= 0, and normalize them
to unit length; scaling does not change either subspace. The same proof applies to
complex Hilbert spaces, although the source says Euclidean.
Theorem 1.7 (Principal cosines for two tensor subspaces [proved here]). Let L be a
real Euclidean or complex Hilbert space of dimension d ≥2. As unit tensors a, b ∈L⊗L

vary, the ordered principal cosines s1 ≥· · · ≥sd ≥0 between a ⊗L and L ⊗b are exactly
the lists satisfying
d
X
j=1
sj ≤1.
For d = 1, the sole possible list is (1).
Proof. In an orthonormal basis write a = P
i,j Aijei ⊗ej and b = P
j,k Bjkej ⊗ek, so ∥A∥F =
∥B∥F = 1. The maps Uu = a ⊗u, V v = v ⊗b are isometries. With the complex inner
product conjugate-linear in its first entry,
(U ∗V )ki = ⟨a ⊗ek, ei ⊗b⟩=
X

AijBjk.
Thus the cross-Gram matrix is (AB)T, and its singular values are the principal cosines.
The trace norm inequality ∥AB∥∗≤∥A∥F ∥B∥F = 1 gives necessity. To check this in-
equality directly, choose a unitary matrix from a singular value decomposition so that
its trace pairing with AB is the sum of the singular values, and apply Cauchy–Schwarz
to the matrix entries. Left or right multiplication by a unitary matrix preserves the
Frobenius norm.
Conversely, put T = P
j sj ≤1, and let r be the number of positive entries. All the
following matrices are real, so their constructions work over either field. If T = 0, take
A11 = B22 = 1, with all other entries zero; both matrices have norm one and AB = 0. If

(1 ≤i ≤r),

It remains to consider r = d. On the open probability simplex xj > 0, P

T = 1, use this minimizing point. If T < 1, the minimum is below one, whereas f(x)
tends to infinity along a segment approaching a face, since every sj > 0. Continuity
supplies a point with f(x) = 1. Now take Ajj = √xj and Bjj = sj/√xj. Both Frobenius
norms equal one, and AB = diag(sj), as required. When d = 1, both tensor subspaces
are the entire one-dimensional ambient space; their principal cosine is one.
Independent verification and scope [computed here].
The cross-Gram formula
was re-derived entry by entry and checked against explicit tensor embeddings, in-
cluding complex coefficients. An independent mathematical review checked all rank
cases and the simplex-continuity argument. The script code/verify_development.py
tests both real and complex random tensors, rank-deficient constructions, and the
exceptional case d = 1; its output records the observed residuals. This resolves the
stated finite-dimensional attainable-range exercise. It does not classify simultaneous
invariants of several unrelated subspaces or the Hilbert-space cases on the next part
of the sheet.
1.39.4
Homology spheres and twisted cohomology: checking a literal
error
This is an annotation of P001475, not an extra class-C or class-D research problem.
The source passes from the Hessian at a flat SU(2) connection to all local systems
and asserts an equivalence with integral first-homology vanishing. At a specified irre-
ducible flat connection with holonomy ρ, the relevant infinitesimal space is H1(M; ad ρ).


CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1987–88
126
Ordinary and twisted homology cannot be interchanged. Already Atiyah (1988, p. 287)
requires perturbations to obtain simple zeros in the homology-sphere construction.
The next two checks disprove both directions of the literal equivalence over charac-
teristic zero.
Lemma 1.8 (Averaging one-cocycles [proved here]). For a finite group G acting lin-
early on a real or complex vector space V , every one-cocycle c : G →V is principal.
Proof. The identity is c(gh) = c(g)+gc(h). Put S = P
h∈G c(h); summing gives S = |G|c(g)+
gS. Thus c(g) = w−gw for w = S/|G|. The characteristic-zero hypothesis permits division
by |G|.
Example 1.9 (Twisted vanishing misses integral torsion [proved here]). The closed
connected orientable manifold M = RP3 = L(2, 1) has π1M = Z/2 and H1(M; Z) = Z/2.
It is not an integral homology sphere. Nevertheless every finite-dimensional real or
complex local system E satisfies
H1(M; E) = H1(π1M; E) = 0
by averaging. The equality in degree one follows because the simply connected uni-
versal cover gives an exact lifted cellular resolution through the degrees needed for
H1. The ordinary homology calculation is also the cellular one in Hatcher (2002, Ex-
amples 2.42–2.43).
Proposition 1.10 (A homology sphere with a degenerate irreducible connection
[proved here]). There is a closed connected orientable integral homology three-sphere
M and an irreducible representation ρ : π1M →SU(2) such that dimR H1(M; ad ρ) = 3.
Proof. Let G = 2I ⊂SU(2) be the binary icosahedral group and Y = S3/G, with the free
orientation-preserving action by left multiplication on unit quaternions. The order-
120 group has presentation ⟨a, b | a5 = b3 = (ab)2⟩. Its abelianization vanishes: taking
the two relators as rows gives the integer matrix
  5 −3
−2 1

, of determinant −1. This
presentation and calculation are recorded in Hatcher (2002, Example 2.38). Hence
H1(Y ; Z) = 0; orientability, connectedness and Poincare duality give the integral ho-
mology of S3.
Set M = Y #Y . Van Kampen gives π1M = G ∗G; abelianization and duality again
give the integral homology of S3. Define ρ to be the inclusion into SU(2) on each free
factor. Its image is nonabelian and acts irreducibly on C2. For V = su2 with the adjoint
action, V G = 0: the icosahedral rotation group has no common fixed axis.
A one-cocycle on G ∗G is uniquely determined by its restrictions to the two free
factors; extend by the cocycle identity along reduced words. Averaging makes these
restrictions w1 −gw1 and w2 −gw2. Since V G = 0, the wi are unique. Thus Z1(G ∗G; V ) ∼=
V ⊕V ; principal cocycles form its diagonal copy of V . Consequently
H1(M; ad ρ) ∼= (V ⊕V )/ diag V ∼= V.
The associated SU(2) principal bundle is trivial, as required in the source: over a
three-dimensional CW complex a section exists because π0, π1, π2 of SU(2) ∼= S3 all
vanish. This verifies the bundle and irreducibility conditions.
Independent second check.
Decompose M into two punctured copies of Y inter-
secting in a collar of S2. With these coefficients each piece has H0 = 0 and H1 = 0, while
the intersection has H0 = V and H1 = 0. Twisted Mayer–Vietoris gives H1(M; V ) ∼= V
again. Puncturing does not change the fundamental group or degree-one local-system
cohomology. This agrees with the cocycle quotient and computes the exact dimension;
it is stronger than merely exhibiting a family of representations.

1.40
Problem index
Earlier-year occurrence identifiers are retained as text; current-year identifiers link
to their entries.
1.40.1
GP-1987-01: Character realizations of Schubert polynomials
For each finite permutation w, find a finite-dimensional polynomial representation, in
a specified group category, whose torus character equals Sw.
Occurrences: 1987-720.21.
Status (2026-11): As of November 2026, the Borel-module formulation is solved by
Kraśkiewicz–Pragacz, in a 1986 preprint announced in 1987 and fully published in
2004. These modules need not be irreducible; the seminar does not specify a category
for its stronger irreducibility wording. (Kraśkiewicz and Pragacz, 2004, Theorem 4.1
and Introduction)
1.40.2
GP-1987-02: Real and complex representability of a finite
matroid
Given a finite matroid by its bases, determine whether it is representable over R or
over C.
Occurrences: 1985-658.13, 1985-658.16, 1987-725.3.
Status (2026-11): As of November 2026, the finite decision problem is settled by
polynomial encoding and quantifier elimination, available before 1987. The broader
phrase classification is not made into a separate unresolved claim without a specified
structural target. This is distinct from earlier problems on realization-space topology
and determinant-sign isotopy. (Tarski, 1951, §§2–3)
1.40.3
GP-1987-03: Axiomatizing the nonzero-minor data of a
bimatroid
Characterize abstract collections R of equally sized row-column subset pairs by ax-
ioms generalizing nonzero square minors.
Occurrences: 1987-725.6.
Status (2026-11): As of November 2026, the collection is a bimatroid exactly when
{(E \ I) ⊔J : (I, J) ∈R} is a matroid basis family on E ⊔F containing E. The checked
modern exposition attributes the theory to Kung 1978. (Röhrle and Ulirsch, 2026,
Proposition 2.2)
1.40.4
GP-1987-04: Information in a bimatroid beyond its row and
column matroids
Determine what data of a matrix bimatroid are lost when only its row matroid and
column matroid are retained.
Occurrences: 1987-725.12.
Status (2026-11): As of November 2026, the separate matroids do not determine the
bimatroid; a two-by-two counterexample is given in the occurrence. Joint submatrix
ranks, equivalently the nonzero square-minor pairs subject to bimatroid axioms, sup-
ply the missing data. (Röhrle and Ulirsch, 2026, Proposition 2.6)

1.40.5
GP-1987-05: Generic irreducible Hecke characters in
symmetric functions
For the semisimple type-A Hecke algebra HN(q), give a symmetric-function formula
for irreducible character values on standard cycle elements Twµ.
Occurrences: 1987-726.8.
Status (2026-11): As of November 2026, a Frobenius formula is available; Ram pub-
lished one in 1991, and Halverson–Ram give the exact normalization and credit sev-
eral independent discoveries. This is a generic-parameter answer and does not settle
the different root-of-unity decomposition problem. (Halverson and Ram, 1999, equa-

1.40.6
GP-1987-21: Joint spectra of row-stochastic matrices
For each n, give necessary and sufficient conditions on a complex multiset (1, λ2, . . . , λn)
to be the spectrum of a real nonnegative n×n matrix with every row sum equal to one.
Occurrences: 1987-728.4.
Status (2026-11): As of 11 November 2026: partly characterized. The order-three
region was known before the talk; recent geometric and prescribed-pattern results
do not give a general explicit solution in orders at least five (Ran and Teng, 2024,
p. 506)(Johnson and Paparella, 2025, Sections 4–5, 8). Section 1.39.1 supplies com-
plete two- and three-state realizations and proves that even all JLL inequalities are
insufficient in order five. Fixed-order algebraic feasibility is decidable, so the unre-
solved goal is a useful structural characterization with realizing matrices, rather than
bare decidability.

2026-11:

1.40.13

dim L = d ≥2, precisely the nonnegative ordered principal cosines of sum at most one
occur; for d=1 only cosine one occurs. The result is presented as a worked solution
of an established linear-algebra exercise, without a novelty claim.

1.40.21

Extend variational differentiation from local scalar functionals to finite sums and prod-

Occurrences: 1987-738.6.

1.40.22
GP-1987-41: Hearts realizing the derived category of a
quiver
Let k be an algebraically closed field and Q a finite acyclic quiver. Describe bounded
t-structures on Db(repk Q) whose heart A is a finite-length abelian k-category with a
projective generator and finite-dimensional Hom spaces, and whose realization func-
tor Db(A) →Db(repk Q) is an equivalence.
Occurrences: 1987-739.3.
Status (2026-11): The Dynkin algebra endpoint was established by Happel (1987, The-
orem 5.12) in 1987, after an Ottawa 1984 announcement. Parthasarathy’s December
1988 publication supplies the precise t-structure-data realization criterion and con-
struction reported here; this later refinement is the class-B publication match. Re-
ceipt on 15 February 1988 is not itself proof of public circulation before the seminar.
Arbitrary embedded abelian subcategories are outside the identified theorem.
1.40.23
GP-1987-42: Fermat’s last theorem
For every integer n > 2, there are no positive integers a, b, c with an+bn = cn. Finiteness
of rational points on a fixed genus-greater-than-one curve does not by itself prove this
nonexistence statement.
Occurrences: 1987-741.3.
Status (2026-11): Settled by the modularity method published in 1995; the 1988
marginal mention of Faltings did not settle the equation.
1.40.24

Does every finite loopless planar graph admit a proper four-colouring?
Occurrences: 1987-741.4, 1987-745.2.
Status (2026-11): Settled by Appel and Haken in 1976, published in 1977; a complete
formal proof was subsequently constructed by Gonthier.

1.40.26
GP-1987-45: Parallel implementation of configuration

Given a finite ring configuration, implement a specified sound finite reducibility test

ity bound or completeness criterion for all notions of reducibility is stated.

1.40.27
GP-1987-46: Microlocal reconstruction of perverse sheaves
Let X be a complex manifold and Λ ⊂T ∗X a specified closed complex analytic conic
Lagrangian. With coefficients in C, describe the category of perverse sheaves with
singular support in Λ by local Morse data on the smooth pieces, gluing at codimension-
one loci, and explicit compatibility conditions at singular loci. A complete answer must
characterize the essential image, not only embed the category faithfully.
Occurrences: 1987-746.4.
Status (2026-11): Partial over complex coefficients: GMV 2005 proves the fully faith-
ful embedding and the codimension-one equivalence; Kashiwara–Vilonen 2014 (an-
nounced 2010, full preprint 2012) proves extension across codimension at least three.
Their Problem 9.3, p. 617, explicitly asks for the geometric codimension-two condi-
tions on the essential image. No general solution to that remaining task was identified
through 11 November 2026.

CHAPTER 1. THE ACADEMIC YEAR 1987–88
145
Evidence. Scan independently checked. RP3 has vanishing twisted H1 over character-
istic zero for every finite-dimensional local system but H1(−; Z) = Z/2. The connected
sum of two Poincare spheres has an irreducible SU(2) adjoint system with first coho-
mology of dimension three; see Section 1.39.4.
P001475 (both; faithful error): Relative-index crossing count
Transcription reads. number of points where a zero mode appears = spectral asym-
metry
Critical reading. spectral flow: signed crossings counted with multiplicity
Evidence. An eigenvalue crossing up and later down contributes zero net spectral flow
but two unsigned events. Atiyah 1988, p. 289, gives the spectral-flow interpretation
and modulo-eight ambiguity.
P001477 (both; faithful error): Dimensions in cutting
Transcription reads. cut a four-manifold into two three-dimensional ones
Critical reading. two four-manifolds with common three-dimensional boundary
Evidence. A codimension-one cut preserves the dimension of each piece; Atiyah 1988,
Section 7, gives the intended decomposition.
P001478-P001479 (both; provenance): Academic-year boundary
Transcription reads. appended scans included in record751 page-range
Critical reading. exclude these pages from the 1987-88 mathematical occurrences
Evidence. P001478 explicitly begins 12 September 1988 with a new Gelfand deter-
minants session; P001479 continues with G. Moore on conformal field theory. Their
inclusion is archival overlap, not continuation of 25 April.
